Reductions in Higher-Order Rewriting and Their Equivalence Thanks: Partially supported by project ECOS-Sud A17C01
Pablo Barenbaum, Eduardo Bonelli

Abstract
. Proof terms are syntactic expressions that represent computations in term rewriting. They were introduced by Meseguer and exploited by van Oostrom and de Vrijer to study equivalence of reductions in (left-linear) first-order term rewriting systems. We study the problem of extending the notion of proof term to higher-order rewriting , which generalizes the first-order setting by allowing terms with binders and higher-order substitution. In previous works that devise proof terms for higher-order rewriting, such as Bruggink’s, it has been noted that the challenge lies in reconciling composition of proof terms and higher-order substitution ( β \beta -equivalence). This led Bruggink to reject “nested” composition, other than at the outermost level. In this paper, we propose a notion of higher-order proof term we dub rewrites that supports nested composition. We then define two notions of equivalence on rewrites, namely permutation equivalence and projection equivalence , and show that they coincide. We also propose a standardization procedure, that computes a canonical representative of the permutation equivalence class of a rewrite.



[a,b]
[c]

1.
Introduction

Term rewriting systems model computation as sequences of steps between terms,

reduction sequences
, where steps are instances of term rewriting
rules
[
Ter03
]
. It is natural to consider reduction sequences up to
swapping of orthogonal steps since such reductions perform the “same work”.
The ensuing notion of equivalence is called
permutation equivalence
and
was first studied by Lévy
[
Lév78
]
in the setting of the

\lambda
-calculus but has appeared in other guises connected with
concurrency
[
Ter03
, Rem.8.1.1]
.
As an example, consider the rewrite rule

\mathbf{c}(x,\mathbf{f}(y))\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{d}(x,x)

and the following reduction sequence
where, in each step, the contracted redex is underlined:

\underline{\mathbf{c}(\mathbf{c}(z,\mathbf{f}(z)),\mathbf{f}(z))}\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{d}(\underline{\mathbf{c}(z,\mathbf{f}(z))},\mathbf{c}(z,\mathbf{f}(z)))\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{d}(\mathbf{d}(z,z),\underline{\mathbf{c}(z,\mathbf{f}(z))})\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{d}(\mathbf{d}(z,z),\mathbf{d}(z,z))

(1)

Performing the innermost redex first, rather than the outermost one, leads to:

\mathbf{c}(\underline{\mathbf{c}(z,\mathbf{f}(z))},\mathbf{f}(z))\rightarrowtriangle\underline{\mathbf{c}(\mathbf{d}(z,z),\mathbf{f}(z))}\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{d}(\mathbf{d}(z,z),\mathbf{d}(z,z))

(2)

The first step in (
1
) makes two copies of the innermost redex. It is the two steps contracting these copies that are swapped with the first one in (
1
) to produce (
2
).
Consider also the following reduction sequence:

\underline{\mathbf{c}(z,\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{c}(z,\mathbf{f}(z))))}\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{d}(z,z)

(3)

Performing the innermost redex first, rather than the outermost one, leads to:

\mathbf{c}(z,\mathbf{f}(\underline{\mathbf{c}(z,\mathbf{f}(z))}))\rightarrowtriangle\underline{\mathbf{c}(z,\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{d}(z,z)))}\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{d}(z,z)

(4)

The step in (
3
) erases the innermost redex. The two steps of (
4
) can be swapped to produce (
3
).
Such duplication and erasure contribute most of the complications behind permutation equivalence, both in its formulation and the study of its properties.

Proof Terms

One natural way to represent computations is by means of so-called
proof terms
.
They were introduced by Meseguer as a means of representing proofs in Rewriting
Logic
[
Mes92
]
and exploited by van Oostrom and
de Vrijer in the setting of first-order left-linear rewriting systems,
to study equivalence of reductions
in
[
vOdV02
]
and
[
Ter03
, Chapter 9]
.
Each rewrite rules is assigned a
rule symbol
denoting the application of that rule. Proof terms are expressions built using
function symbols, rule symbols, and a binary operator “
\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}
”
denoting sequential composition of proof terms.
Assuming
that we choose the symbol “
\varrho
” for our rewrite rule,
in such a way that
\varrho(x,y)
witnesses the step

\mathbf{c}(x,\mathbf{f}(y))\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{d}(x,x)
,
reduction (
1
) may be represented
as the proof term:

\varrho(\mathbf{c}(z,\mathbf{f}(z)),z)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathbf{d}(\varrho(z,z),\mathbf{c}(z,\mathbf{f}(z)))\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathbf{d}(\mathbf{d}(z,z),\varrho(z,z))

and reduction (
2
) as the proof term:

\mathbf{c}(\varrho(z,z),\mathbf{f}(z))\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\varrho(\mathbf{d}(z,z),z)
.
One notable feature of proof terms is that they support parallel steps. For instance, both proof terms above are permutation equivalent
to

\varrho(\mathbf{c}(z,\mathbf{f}(z)),z)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathbf{d}(\varrho(z,z),\varrho(z,z))
,
which performs the two last steps in parallel,
as well as to

\varrho(\varrho(z,z),z)
,
which performs all steps simultaneously.
Using this representation, permutation equivalence
can be studied in terms of equational theories on proof terms.

Equivalence of Reductions via Proof Terms for First-Order Rewriting.

In
[
vOdV02
]
,
van Oostrom and de Vrijer characterize permutation equivalence
of proof terms in four alternative ways.
First, they formulate an equational theory
of permutation equivalence
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma

between proof terms, such that for example

\varrho(\mathbf{c}(z,\mathbf{f}(z)),z)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathbf{d}(\varrho(z,z),\varrho(z,z))\mathrel{\approx}\varrho(\varrho(z,z),z)
holds.
These equations account for the behavior of proof term composition,
which has a monoidal structure, in the sense that composition is associative
and
empty
steps act as identities.
Second, they define an operation of
projection

\rho/\sigma
,
denoting the computational work that is left of
\rho
after

\sigma
.
For example,

\mathbf{c}(\varrho(z,z),\mathbf{f}(z))/\varrho(\mathbf{c}(z,\mathbf{f}(z)),z)=\mathbf{d}(\varrho(z,z),\varrho(z,z))
.
This induces a notion of
projection equivalence

between proof terms
\rho
and
\sigma
,
declared to hold when both
\rho/\sigma
and
\sigma/\rho

are empty,
i.e.
they contain no rule symbols.
Third, they define a
standardization procedure
to reorder the
steps of a reduction in outside-in order, mapping each proof term
\rho

to a proof term
\rho^{*}
in
standard form
.
For example, the (parallel) standard form of

\mathbf{c}(\varrho(z,z),\mathbf{f}(z))\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\varrho(\mathbf{d}(z,z),z)

is

\varrho(\mathbf{c}(z,\mathbf{f}(z)),z)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathbf{d}(\varrho(z,z),\varrho(z,z))
.
This induces a notion of
standardization equivalence

between proof terms
\rho
and
\sigma
,
declared to hold when
\rho^{*}=\sigma^{*}
.
Fourth, they define a notion of
labelling equivalence
,
based on lifting computational steps to labelled terms.
Although these notions of equivalence were known prior to
[
vOdV02
]
,
the main
contribution of that paper is to show that they can be systematically studied using proof terms
and, moreover, shown to coincide.

Higher-Order Rewriting.

Higher-order term rewriting (HOR) generalizes first-order term rewriting
by allowing binders.
Function symbols
are generalized to constants of any given simple type,
and first-order terms
are generalized to simply-typed
\lambda
-terms,
including constants and up to
\beta\eta
-equivalence.
The paradigmatic example of a higher-order rewriting system is the

\lambda
-calculus.
It includes a base type
\iota

and two constants
\mathbf{app}:\iota\to\iota\to\iota

and
\mathbf{lam}:(\iota\to\iota)\to\iota
;
\beta
-reduction
may be expressed as
the higher-order rewrite rule

\mathbf{app}\,(\mathbf{lam}\,(\lambda z.x\,z))\,y\rightarrowtriangle x\,y
.
A sample reduction sequence is:

\mathbf{lam}(\lambda v.\mathbf{app}(\mathbf{lam}(\lambda x.x),\underline{\mathbf{app}(\mathbf{lam}(\lambda w.w),v)}))\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{lam}(\lambda v.\underline{\mathbf{app}(\mathbf{lam}(\lambda x.x),v)})\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{lam}(\lambda v.v)

(5)

Generalizing proof terms to the setting of higher-order rewriting is a natural
goal. Just like in the first-order case, we assign rule symbols to rewrite rules. One would then expect to obtain proof terms by adding these rule symbols and the “
\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}
” composition operator to the simply typed
\lambda
-calculus. If we assume the following rule symbol for our rewrite rule
\varrho\,x\,y:\mathbf{app}\,(\mathbf{lam}\,(\lambda z.x\,z))\,y\rightarrowtriangle x\,y
, then an example of a higher-order proof term for (
5
) is:

{\small\mathbf{lam}\bigg(\lambda v.\big(\mathbf{app}(\mathbf{lam}(\lambda x.x),\varrho\,(\lambda w.w)\,v)\ ;\ \varrho\,(\lambda u.u)\,v\big)\bigg)}

However, higher-order substitution and proof term composition seem not to be in
consonance, an issue already observed by Bruggink
[
Bru08
]
.
Consider a variable
x
. This variable itself
denotes an empty computation
x\rightarrowtriangle x
,
so the composition
(x\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}x)

also denotes an empty computation
x\rightarrowtriangle x
.
If
\sigma
is an arbitrary proof term representing a computation
s\rightarrowtriangle t
,
the proof term
(\lambda x.(x\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}x))\,\sigma

should, in principle, represent a computation

(\lambda x.x)\,s\rightarrowtriangle(\lambda x.x)\,t
.
This is the same as
s\rightarrowtriangle t
,
because terms are regarded up to
\beta\eta
-equivalence.
The challenge lies in lifting
\beta\eta
-equivalence to the level of
proof terms:
if
\beta
-reduction is naively extended to operate on proof terms,
the well-formed proof term
(\lambda x.(x\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}x))\,\sigma

becomes equal to
(\sigma\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma)
,
which is ill-formed because
\sigma
is not composable with itself
if
s\not=_{\beta\eta}t
.
Rather than simply disallowing the use of “
\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}
” under applications and
abstractions (the route taken in
[
Bru08
]
), our aim is to integrate it with

\beta\eta
-reduction.

Contribution.

We propose a syntax for higher-order proof terms,
called
rewrites
, that includes
\beta\eta
-equivalence
and allows them to be freely composed.
We then define a relation
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma

of
permutation equivalence
between rewrites, the central notion of our work.
The issue mentioned above is avoided by
disallowing

the ill-behaved substitution
of a rewrite in a rewrite “
\rho\{x\backslash\sigma\}
”,
and by only allowing notions of substitution
of a term in a rewrite
\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}
,
and of a rewrite in a term
s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.
From these, a well-behaved
notion of substitution of a rewrite in a rewrite

\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}

can be shown to be
derivable
.
We also define a notion of
projection

\rho{/\!\!/}\sigma
.
The induced notion of

projection equivalence coincides with permutation equivalence
,
in the sense that

\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma
iff

\rho{/\!\!/}\sigma\mathrel{\approx}\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}}

and

\sigma{/\!\!/}\rho\mathrel{\approx}\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}
,
where
\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}
stands for the
target
term of
\rho
.
The equivalence is established by means of
flattening
,
a method to convert an arbitrary rewrite
\rho

into a (
flat
) representative
\rho^{\mathcal{F}}

that only uses the composition operator “
\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}
” at the top level, and
a notion of
flat permutation equivalence

\rho\mathrel{\sim}\sigma
.
Flattening is achieved by means of a rewriting system
whose objects are themselves rewrites.
This system is shown to be confluent and strongly normalizing. We also show that

permutation equivalence is sound and complete
with respect to flat permutation equivalence

in the sense that
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma

if and only if
\rho^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{\sim}\sigma^{\mathcal{F}}
.
Finally, we present a notion of parallel standardization
that can be used to effectively
find a canonical representative
\rho^{*}
of the permutation equivalence class
of a given rewrite
\rho
, under appropriate conditions.

Structure of the Paper.

In Section
2

we review Nipkow’s Higher-Order Rewriting Systems.
Section
3
proposes our notion of rewrite and Section
4

introduces permutation equivalence for them.
Flattening is presented in Section
5
.
In this section, we also formulate an equational theory
defining the relation
\rho\mathrel{\sim}\sigma

of flat permutation equivalence between flat rewrites.
It relies crucially on a ternary relation
between
multisteps
, called
splitting

and written
\mu\Leftrightarrow\mu_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mu_{2}
,
meaning that
\mu
and
\mu_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mu_{2}

perform the same computational work.
In Section
6

we first define a projection operator for flat rewrites
\rho/\sigma
,
and we lift it to a projection operator for arbitrary rewrites

\rho{/\!\!/}\sigma\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\rho^{\mathcal{F}}/\sigma^{\mathcal{F}}
.
Then we show that
the induced notion of projection equivalence coincides with permutation equivalence.
Section
7
presents some preliminary results on parallel standardization
of rewrites.
Finally, we conclude and discuss related and future work.

Note.

An extended abstract of this paper was published as
[
BB23
]
.
This report includes full proofs and a new section on Standardization (Section
7
).

2.
Higher-Order Rewriting

There are various approaches to HOR in the literature,
including Klop’s Combinatory Reduction Systems (CRSs)
[
Klo80
]

and Nipkow’s Higher-Order Rewriting Systems
(HRSs)
[
Nip91
,
MN98
]
. We consider HRSs in this paper.
Their use of the simply-typed lambda calculus for representing terms and
substitution provides a suitable starting point for modeling our rewrites.

Assume given a denumerably infinite set of

variables
(
x,y,\ldots
),

base types
(
\alpha,\beta,\ldots
), and

constant symbols
(
\mathbf{c},\mathbf{d},\ldots
).
The sets of
terms
(
s,t,\ldots
) and

types
(
A,B,\ldots
)
are given by:

\begin{array}[]{lrl@{\hspace{1cm}}lrl}s&::=&x\,\mid\,\mathbf{c}\,\mid\,\lambda x.s\,\mid\,s\,s\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&A&::=&\alpha\,\mid\,A\to A\\
\end{array}

A term can either be a variable, a constant, an abstraction or an
application. A type can either be a base type or an arrow type. We write
\mathsf{fv}(s)
for the free variables of
s
.
We use
\overline{X_{n}}
, or sometimes just
\overline{X}
if
n
is clear
from the context,
to denote a sequence
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
.
Following standard conventions,
s\,\overline{t_{n}}
stands for the iterated application

s\,t_{1}\ldots t_{n}
,
and
\overline{A_{n}}\to B
for the type
A_{1}\to\ldots A_{n}\to B
.
We write
s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}
for the capture-avoiding
substitution of all free occurrences of
x
in
s
with
t
and call it a
term/term substitution
.
As usual, terms are defined modulo
\alpha
-conversion,
i.e.
we identify terms that differ only in the names of their bound variables. A
typing context
(
\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},\ldots
) is a partial function from variables to types. We write
\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
for the
domain
of
\Gamma
. Given a typing context
\Gamma
and
x\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
, we write
\Gamma,x:A
for the typing context such that
(\Gamma,x:A)(x)=A
, and
(\Gamma,x:A)(y)=\Gamma(y)
whenever
y\neq x
. We write a centered dot

\cdot
for the empty typing context and

x\in\Gamma
if
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
.
A
signature
of a HRS is a set
\mathcal{C}
of typed constants
\mathbf{c}:A
. A sample signature is
\mathcal{C}=\{\mathbf{app}:\iota\to\iota\to\iota,\mathbf{lam}:(\iota\to\iota)\to\iota\}
for
\iota
a base type.

{defi}

[Type system for terms]

Terms are typed using the usual typing rules of the simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty(x:A)\in\Gamma$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=47.79842pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{Var}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 8.33325pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}x}:A$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty(\mathbf{c}:A)\in\mathcal{C}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=42.61102pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{Con}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 6.04166pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathbf{c}}:A$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 4.02779pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:B$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=64.73941pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{Abs}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\lambda x.s}:A\to B$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:A\to B\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=92.0005pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{App}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 28.01591pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s\,t}:B$}}}}\end{array}

Given any
\Gamma
and
A
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:A
can be proved
using these rules, we say
s
is a
typed term
over
\mathcal{C}
.
We typically assume
\mathcal{C}
to be globally fixed, leaving it as an implicit parameter of the type system.

The notions
of
\beta
and
\eta
-reduction between terms are defined as usual.
Recall that
\beta
-reduction and
\eta
-reduction are confluent and terminating on typed terms.
We write
{s}\downarrow^{\beta}
(resp.
{s}\downarrow^{\eta}
) for the unique
\beta
-normal form (resp.
\eta
-normal form) of
s
. The
\beta
-normal form of a term
s
has the form
\lambda\overline{x_{k}}.{a\,t_{1}\ldots t_{m}}
, for
a
either a constant or a variable. The
\eta
-expanded form of
s
is defined as:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{s}\uparrow^{\eta}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\lambda\overline{x_{n+k}}.{a\,({\overline{t_{m}}}\uparrow^{\eta})\,({x_{n+1}}\uparrow^{\eta})\ldots({x_{n+k}}\uparrow^{\eta})}\end{array}

where
s
is assumed to have type
\overline{A_{n+k}}\to B
and the
x_{n+1},\ldots,x_{n+k}
are fresh. We use
{s}\updownarrow^{\eta}_{\beta}
to denote the term
{{s}\downarrow^{\beta}}\uparrow^{\eta}
and call it the
\beta\overline{\eta}
-normal form of
s
.

A
substitution

\theta
is a function mapping variables to typed terms
such that
\theta(x)\neq x
only for finitely many
x
.
The
domain
of a substitution is defined as

\mathsf{dom}(\theta)=\{x\ |\ \theta(x)\neq x\}
.
The application of a substitution
\theta=\{x_{1}\mapsto s_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\mapsto s_{n}\}

to a term
t
is defined as
\theta\,t\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,{((\lambda\overline{x_{n}}.{t})\,\overline{s_{n}})}\updownarrow^{\eta}_{\beta}
.

{defi}

A
pattern
is a typed term in
\beta
-normal form
such that all free occurrences of a variable
x_{i}
are in a subterm of the
form
x_{i}\,t_{1}\ldots t_{k}

with
t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}

\eta
-equivalent to
distinct bound variables.
A
rewriting rule
is a pair

\langle\ell,r\rangle

of typed terms in
\beta\overline{\eta}
-normal form of the same base type with
\ell

a pattern not
\eta
-equivalent to a variable and
\mathsf{fv}(r)\subseteq\mathsf{fv}(\ell)
. An HRS is a pair consisting of a signature and a set of rewriting rules over that signature. We typically omit the signature.

{exa}

Consider a base type
\iota
and typed constants

\mathbf{mu}:(\iota\to\iota)\to\iota
and
\mathbf{f}:\iota\to\iota
. Two sample rewriting rules are:

\langle\mathbf{mu}(\lambda y.x\,y),x\,(\mathbf{mu}(\lambda y.x\,y))\rangle

and

\langle\mathbf{f}\,x,\mathbf{g}\,x\rangle
. All four terms have base type
\iota
.

{defi}

The rewrite relation
\mathrel{\,\to_{\mathcal{R}}\,}
for an HRS
\mathcal{R}
is the relation over typed terms in
\beta\overline{\eta}
-normal form defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 1.94434pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\langle\ell,r\rangle\in\mathcal{R}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=39.09474pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{Root}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\theta\,\ell\mathrel{\,\to_{\mathcal{R}}\,}\theta\,r$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 20.2858pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty s\mathrel{\,\to_{\mathcal{R}}\,}t$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=66.28676pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{App}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle a\,\overline{r_{m}}\,s\,\overline{p_{n}}\mathrel{\,\to_{\mathcal{R}}\,}a\,\overline{r_{m}}\,t\,\overline{p_{n}}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 15.99304pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty s\mathrel{\,\to_{\mathcal{R}}\,}t$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=57.70125pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{Abs}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lambda x.s\mathrel{\,\to_{\mathcal{R}}\,}\lambda x.t$}}}}\end{array}

where
a
is either a constant or a variable of type
\overline{A_{m+1+n}}\to B
.
We write
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}}
(resp.
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}}
) for the reflexive, transitive (resp. reflexive, symmetric and transitive) closure of
\mathrel{\,\to_{\mathcal{R}}\,}
.

{exa}

An example of a sequence of rewrite steps in the HRS of Example
2
is

\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x)\mathrel{\,\to_{\mathcal{R}}\,}\mathbf{f}\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x))\mathrel{\,\to_{\mathcal{R}}\,}\mathbf{f}\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x))\mathrel{\,\to_{\mathcal{R}}\,}\mathbf{g}\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x))
.

Recall that an HRS is
orthogonal
if:

1.

The rules are
left-linear
,
i.e.
if the left-hand side

\ell
has
\mathsf{fv}(\ell)=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
,
then
there is
exactly
one free occurrence of
x_{i}
in
\ell
, for each
1\leq i\leq n
.

2.

There are
no critical pairs
, as defined for example in
[
Nip91
, Def. 4.1]
.

Orthogonal HRSs are deterministic in the sense that their rewrite relation is
confluent
[
Nip91
]
.
All of the examples of HRSs presented above are orthogonal. In the
sequel of this paper, we assume given a fixed, orthogonal HRS
\mathcal{R}
.

3.
Rewrites

In this section we propose a syntax for higher-order proof terms,
called
rewrites
1
1

1

Our notion of rewrite is unrelated to that of Definition 2.4 in
[
Mes92
]
; it corresponds to “proof terms” as introduced in Section 3.1 in
[
Mes92
]
.
. Rewrites for an HRS
\mathcal{R}
are a means for denoting proofs in Higher-Order Rewriting Logic (HORL,
cf.
Definition
3
) which, in turn, correspond to reduction sequences in
\mathcal{R}
(
cf.
Theorem.
2
). As in the first-order case
[
Mes92
]
, HORL is simply the equational theory that results from an HRS but disregarding symmetry.
Given an HRS
\mathcal{R}
, let
{\mathcal{R}}^{c}
denote the set of pairs
\langle\lambda\overline{x_{n}}.\ell,\lambda\overline{x_{n}}.r\rangle
such that
\langle\ell,r\rangle\in\mathcal{R}
and
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}=\mathsf{fv}(\ell)
. We begin by recalling the definition of equational logic (
cf.
Definition
3
), the equational theory induced by an HRS. It is essentially that of
[
MN98
, Def. 3.11]
, except that in the inference rule
ERule
we use
{\mathcal{R}}^{c}
rather than
\mathcal{R}
. This equivalent formulation will be convenient when introducing rewrites since free variables in the LHS of a rewrite rule will be reflected in the rewrite too.

{defi}

[Equational Logic]

An HRS
\mathcal{R}
induces a congruence

{}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{}
on terms defined by the following rules:

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 1.02179pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:B\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=95.4454pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{EBeta}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}(\lambda x.s)\,t}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}:B$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:B\hskip 14.22636ptx\notin\mathsf{fv}(s)$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=99.7095pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{EEta}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 16.382pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\lambda x.s\,x}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:B$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 1.3207pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty(x:A)\in\Gamma$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=46.80914pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{EVar}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}x}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}x}:A$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 3.18524pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty(\mathbf{c}:A)\in\mathcal{C}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=45.78963pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{ECon}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathbf{c}}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathbf{c}}:A$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}:A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=53.64098pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{ESymm}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}:A$}}}}\end{array}\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}:A\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{2}}:A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=121.50832pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{ETrans}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 33.93367pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{2}}:A$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 11.10063pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}:B$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=100.35214pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{EAbs}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\lambda x.s_{0}}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\lambda x.s_{1}}:A\to B$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}:A\to B\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t_{0}}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t_{1}}:A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=136.78214pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{EApp}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 32.12466pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}\,t_{0}}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}\,t_{1}}:B$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\langle s,t\rangle\in{\mathcal{R}}^{c}\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cdot}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:A\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cdot}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=117.34286pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{ERule}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 36.68527pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A$}}}}\end{array}\end{array}

There is no explicit reflexivity rule saying that

s{}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{}s
. But note that this is easy to prove
by induction on
s
.

Theorem 1

(Thm. 3.12 in
[
MN98
]
)
.

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
iff
{s}\updownarrow^{\eta}_{\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}}{t}\updownarrow^{\eta}_{\beta}
.

The
(\Leftarrow)
direction follows from observing that
\rightarrow_{\beta,\overline{\eta}}
and
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}}
are all included in
\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}
. The
(\Rightarrow)
direction is by induction on the derivation of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\doteq_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
.

Higher-Order Rewriting Logic results from removing the
ESymm
rule
in Definition
3
and adding a proof witness. Its judgments take the form

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
where

\rho
is an expression that witnesses
the proof of the judgment, called a
rewrite
. Given a set of
rule symbols

(
\varrho,\vartheta,\ldots
), the set of
rewrites

(
\rho,\sigma,\ldots
) is defined by the grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\rho::=x\,\mid\,\mathbf{c}\,\mid\,\varrho\,\mid\,\lambda x.\rho\,\mid\,\rho\,\rho\,\mid\,\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho\end{array}

A rewrite can either be a variable, a constant, a rule symbol,
an abstraction congruence, an application congruence, or a composition.
Note that composition may occur anywhere inside a rewrite
2
2

2

This is
one of the key differences between our approach and that of
[
Bru08
]
.
.
For the sake of clarity we present the full system for Higher-Order Rewriting Logic next. We assume given an HRS
\mathcal{R}
such that each rewrite rule
\langle\ell,r\rangle\in\mathcal{R}
has been assigned a unique rule symbol
\varrho
and shall write
\langle\varrho,\ell,r\rangle\in\mathcal{R}
and also use the same notation for
{\mathcal{R}}^{c}
. HORL consists of two forms of typing judgments:

1.

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
,
meaning that

s
and
t
are
\beta\eta
-equivalent
terms of type
A
under
\Gamma
;
and

2.

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
,
meaning that
\rho
is a rewrite
with source
s
and target
t
,
which are terms of type
A
under
\Gamma
.

{defi}

[Higher-Order Rewriting Logic]

Term equivalence is defined as follows:

{\small\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 2.68884pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:B\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=98.77951pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{EqBeta}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}(\lambda x.s)\,t}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}:B$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:B\hskip 14.22636ptx\notin\mathsf{fv}(s)$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=99.7095pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{EqEta}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 14.71495pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\lambda x.s\,x}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:B$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 10.22903pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:A$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=48.27522pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{EqRefl}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:A$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=47.30646pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{EqSym}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:A$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r}:A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=108.96082pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{EqTrans}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 30.28204pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r}:A$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 11.10063pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}:B$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=103.68626pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{EqCongAbs}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\lambda x.s_{0}}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\lambda x.s_{1}}:A\to B$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}:A\to B\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t_{0}}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t_{1}}:A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=143.45038pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{EqCongApp}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 34.04744pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}\,t_{0}}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}\,t_{1}}:A$}}}}\end{array}\end{array}}

Typing rules for rewrites are as follows:

{\small\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 5.22693pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty(x:A)\in\Gamma$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=54.62161pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{RVar}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}x}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}x}:A$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 6.8366pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty(\mathbf{c}:A)\in\mathcal{C}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=53.09233pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{RCon}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\mathbf{c}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathbf{c}}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathbf{c}}:A$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 18.47559pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}:B$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=122.4364pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{RAbs}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\lambda x.\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\lambda x.s_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\lambda x.s_{1}}:A\to B$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}:A\to B\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t_{1}}:A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=151.9751pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{RApp}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 32.65938pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho\,\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}\,t_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}\,t_{1}}:B$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\langle\varrho,s,t\rangle\in{\mathcal{R}}^{c}\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cdot}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:A\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cdot}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=126.21887pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{RRule}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 37.4561pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\varrho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}:A\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{2}}:A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=136.70128pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{RTrans}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 33.73923pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{2}}:A$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t^{\prime}}:A\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}:A\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t^{\prime}}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=185.58894pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{RConv}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 67.14113pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A$}}}}\end{array}\end{array}}

Term equivalence rules (prefixed by “
Eq
”) define
the least congruence generated by
\beta
and
\eta
-conversion
over simply typed
\lambda
-terms.
Typing rules for rewrites (prefixed by “
R
”) include
the
RVar
and
RCon
rules,
which express that variables and constants represent identity rewrites.
The
RAbs
and
RApp
rules
express congruence below abstraction and application.
The
RRule
rule allows to use a rule symbol to stand for
a rewrite between its source and its target,
which must be closed terms of the same type.
The
RTrans
rule is the typing rule for rewrite composition,
which can be understood as expressing that the rewriting relation is closed by
transitivity.
The
RConv
rule states that the source and the target
of a rewrite are regarded up to
\beta\eta
-equivalence.
Note that there are no rules equating rewrites; such rules are the purpose of Section
4
which introduces permutation equivalence.

{exa}

Suppose we assign the following rule symbols to the rewriting rules of Example
2
:

\langle\varrho,\mathbf{mu}(\lambda y.x\,y),x\,(\mathbf{mu}(\lambda y.x\,y))\rangle

and

\langle\vartheta,\mathbf{f}\,x,\mathbf{g}\,x\rangle
.
Recall that
\mathcal{C}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\{\mathbf{mu}:(\iota\to\iota)\to\iota,\mathbf{f}:\iota\to\iota\}
.
The reduction sequence of Example
2
can be represented as a rewrite:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\cdot}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\varrho\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathbf{f}\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\vartheta\,x))\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\vartheta\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x))}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x)}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathbf{g}\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x)}:\iota

Inspection of the proof of Theorem
1
in
[
MN98
]
reveals that
\beta
and
\eta
are only needed for substitutions in rewrite rules. As a consequence:

Theorem 2
.

There is a rewrite
\rho
such that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
if and only if
{s}\updownarrow^{\eta}_{\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}}{t}\updownarrow^{\eta}_{\beta}
.

Now that we know that rewrites over an HRS
\mathcal{R}
are sound and complete with respect to reduction sequences in
\mathcal{R}
, we review some basic properties of rewrites and then focus, in the remaining sections, on equivalences between rewrites. In the sequel we will omit
\mathcal{R}
in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
and write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
.

3.1.
Properties of Rewrites

We begin by introducing the notion of source and target of a rewrite and then move on to a few important syntactic properties of terms and rewrites.

{defi}
[Source and target of a rewrite]
For each rewrite
\rho

we define the
source

\rho^{\mathsf{src}}

and the
target

\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}

as the following terms:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}x^{\mathsf{src}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&x\\
\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{src}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathbf{c}\\
\varrho^{\mathsf{src}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&s\hskip 14.22636pt\text{if $(\varrho:s\rightarrowtriangle t:A)\in\mathcal{R}$}\\
(\lambda x.\rho)^{\mathsf{src}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\lambda x.\rho^{\mathsf{src}}\\
(\rho\,\sigma)^{\mathsf{src}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\rho^{\mathsf{src}}\,\sigma^{\mathsf{src}}\\
(\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma)^{\mathsf{src}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\rho^{\mathsf{src}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{rcll}x^{\mathsf{tgt}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&x\\
\mathbf{c}^{\mathsf{tgt}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathbf{c}\\
\varrho^{\mathsf{tgt}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&t\hskip 14.22636pt\text{if $(\varrho:s\rightarrowtriangle t:A)\in\mathcal{R}$}\\
(\lambda x.\rho)^{\mathsf{tgt}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\lambda x.\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}\\
(\rho\,\sigma)^{\mathsf{tgt}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}\,\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}}\\
(\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma)^{\mathsf{tgt}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}\end{array}

The free variables of
an expression
X
(which may be a term or a rewrite) are written
\mathsf{fv}(X)
,
and defined as expected, with lambdas binding variables
in their bodies.
We write
\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}

for the capture-avoiding substitution of
the variable
x
in the rewrite
\rho
by
t
.
This operation is called
rewrite/term substitution
.
Together with term/term substitution
s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}
(
cf.
Section
2
)
this allows us to write
X{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}
when
X
is either a term or a rewrite.

As for the properties, we begin with the
Substitution Lemma
, which holds for any term or rewrite
X

as long as
x\neq y
and
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)
and is proved by induction on
X
:

Lemma 3
.

The equation

X{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}y{\backslash}t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}=X{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}y{\backslash}t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}y{\backslash}t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}

holds.

Weakening
holds too, proved by induction on the derivation of the target judgment:

Lemma 4
.

Let
x\notin\Gamma
. Then:

1.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:A

then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:B}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:A
.

2.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A

then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:B}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
.

3.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A

then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:B}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
.

Substitution of a variable by a term
commutes with the source and target operators
:

Lemma 5
.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:B

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r}:A

then

\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}^{\mathsf{src}}=\rho^{\mathsf{src}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}

and
\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}^{\mathsf{tgt}}=\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

Proof 3.1
.

By induction on
\rho
.

Substitution also
preserves typing
.
In particular, the third item of the following lemma
expresses that rewrite/terms substitution can
be understood, intuitively,
as the morphism component of a functor
\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}
:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 36.7848pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty s\overset{\rho}{\xrightarrow{\hskip 28.45274pt}}t$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=121.00815pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\overset{\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}{\xrightarrow{\hskip 28.45274pt}}t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}$}}}}\end{array}

Lemma 6
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r}:A
. Then:

1.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:B

then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}:B
.

2.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:B

then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}:B
.

3.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:B

then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}:B
.

Proof 3.2
.

Each item is by induction on the derivation of the target judgment.

Terms appearing in valid equality and rewriting judgments
are typable:

Lemma 7
.

1.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A

then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:A

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
.

2.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A

then

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:A

and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
.

Proof 3.3
.

The first item is by induction on the derivation of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
and the second by induction on the derivation of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
.

Given a typable rewrite,

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
,
the source of
\rho
and
s
are not necessarily equal,
but they are interconvertible, and similarly for the target:

Lemma 8
.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A

then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho^{\mathsf{src}}}:A

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}}:A
.

Proof 3.4
.

By induction on the derivation of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
.

For example,
if
\varrho:\lambda x.\mathbf{c}\,x\rightarrowtriangle\lambda x.\mathbf{d}:A\to A

then it can be shown that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\varrho\,\mathbf{d}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathbf{c}\,\mathbf{d}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathbf{d}}:A
,
and indeed

\mathbf{c}\,\mathbf{d}=_{\beta\eta}(\lambda x.\mathbf{c}\,x)\,\mathbf{d}=(\varrho\,\mathbf{d})^{\mathsf{src}}
.

Any typable term
s
can be understood as an empty or
unit

rewrite
\underline{s}
,
without occurrences of rule symbols, between
s
and itself. This allows us to coerce terms to rewrites implicitly
if there is little danger of confusion.

Lemma 9
.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:A

then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\underline{s}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:A
.

Proof 3.5
.

Straightforward by induction on the derivation of the judgment.

Term/rewrite substitution
, defined below, generalizes
term/term substitution
s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}

when
t
is a rewrite,
i.e.

s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.
Sometimes we also call this notion
lifting substitution
,
as
s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}
“lifts”
the expression
s
from the level of terms to the level of rewrites.

{defi}

[Term/rewrite substitution]

\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\HS}rcl}y{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\begin{cases}\rho&\text{if $x=y$}\\
y&\text{if $x\neq y$}\end{cases}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\mathbf{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\mathbf{c}\\
(\lambda y.s){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\lambda y.s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{if $x\neq y$}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&(s\,t){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\,t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\end{array}

We mention some important properties of term/rewrite substitution. First, rewrite/term substitution commutes with source and target operations:

Lemma 10
.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r}:B

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A

then

r{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}^{\mathsf{src}}=r{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho^{\mathsf{src}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}

and
r{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}^{\mathsf{tgt}}=r{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

Proof 3.6
.

By induction on
r
.

Second, term/rewrite substitution can
be understood, intuitively,
as the morphism component of a functor
r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}
:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 37.66676pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty s\overset{\rho}{\xrightarrow{\hskip 28.45274pt}}t$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=122.77206pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\overset{r{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}}{\xrightarrow{\hskip 28.45274pt}}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}$}}}}\end{array}

More precisely:

Lemma 11
.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r}:B

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A

then

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}r{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}:B
.

Proof 3.7
.

By induction on the derivation of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r}:B
.

Third, term/rewrite and rewrite/term substitution commute as follows:

Lemma 12
.

If

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A,y:B}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:C

and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,y:B}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\tau}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p_{1}}:A

and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}q}:B

then:

s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\tau{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}y{\backslash}q{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}y{\backslash}q{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\tau{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}y{\backslash}q{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}

In particular, if
y
does not occur free in
\tau
,
then

s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\tau{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}y{\backslash}q{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}y{\backslash}q{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\tau{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

Proof 3.8
.

By induction on the derivation of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A,y:B}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:C
.

It is also easy to show that term/rewrite substitution commutes with reflexivity
in the sense that
\underline{s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}=s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\underline{t}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}
,
and with source and target operators,
in the sense that

s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}^{\mathsf{src}}=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\rho^{\mathsf{src}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}

and
s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}^{\mathsf{tgt}}=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

4.
Permutation equivalence

This section presents
permutation equivalence
(Definition
4
),
an equivalence relation over (typed) rewrites
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma
that identifies any
two rewrites
\rho
and
\sigma

—in a given orthogonal HRS
\mathcal{R}
—
when they denote computations that, intuitively speaking,
perform the “same computational work”.
In Section
5
, we will show
that, as the name suggests, permutation equivalence essentially amounts
to identifying computations up to permutation of steps.

{defi}

[Permutation equivalence]

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t^{\prime}}:A
are derivable. Permutation equivalence, written
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash({\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t})\mathrel{\approx}({\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t^{\prime}}):A
(or simply
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\rho^{\prime}

if
\Gamma,s,t,s^{\prime},t^{\prime},A
are clear from the context),
is defined as the reflexive, symmetric, transitive, and contextual closure
of the following axioms:

\begin{array}[]{rclrl}\underline{\rho^{\mathsf{src}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho&\mathrel{\approx}&\rho&&\textsf{{$\mathrel{\approx}$-IdL}}\\
\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\underline{\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}}&\mathrel{\approx}&\rho&&\textsf{{$\mathrel{\approx}$-IdR}}\\
(\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tau&\mathrel{\approx}&\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\sigma\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tau)&&\textsf{{$\mathrel{\approx}$-Assoc}}\\
(\lambda x.\rho)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\lambda x.\sigma)&\mathrel{\approx}&\lambda x.(\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma)&&\textsf{{$\mathrel{\approx}$-Abs}}\\
(\rho_{1}\rho_{2})\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2})&\mathrel{\approx}&(\rho_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma_{1})(\rho_{2}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma_{2})&&\textsf{{$\mathrel{\approx}$-App}}\\
(\lambda x.\underline{s})\,\rho&\mathrel{\approx}&s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}&&\textsf{{$\mathrel{\approx}$-BetaTR}}\\
(\lambda x.\rho)\,\underline{s}&\mathrel{\approx}&\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}&&\textsf{{$\mathrel{\approx}$-BetaRT}}\\
\lambda x.\rho\,x&\mathrel{\approx}&\rho&\text{if $x\notin\mathsf{fv}(\rho)$}&\textsf{{$\mathrel{\approx}$-Eta}}\end{array}

Rules
\mathrel{\approx}
-IdL
,
\mathrel{\approx}
-IdR
and
\mathrel{\approx}
-Assoc
, state
that rewrites together with rewrite composition have a monoidal structure.
Recall from Section
3
that
\rho^{\mathsf{src}}
is a term and

\underline{\rho^{\mathsf{src}}}
is its corresponding rewrite. Rules
\mathrel{\approx}
-Abs

and
\mathrel{\approx}
-App
state that rewrite composition
commutes
with abstraction and application.
An important thing to be wary of is that rules
may be applied only if both the left and the right-hand sides are well-typed.
In particular, the right-hand side of the
\mathrel{\approx}
-App
rule may
not be well-typed even if the left-hand side is; for example
given rule symbols

\mathbf{c}:A\to B
and
\mathbf{d}:A
,
the expression
((\lambda x.x)(\mathbf{c}\,\mathbf{d}))\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\mathbf{c}\,\mathbf{d})

is well-typed, with source and target
\mathbf{c}\,\mathbf{d}
,
while
{((\lambda x.x)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathbf{c})\,((\mathbf{c}\,\mathbf{d})\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathbf{d})}

is not well-typed.
Finally, rules
\mathrel{\approx}
-BetaTR
,

\mathrel{\approx}
-BetaRT
and
\mathrel{\approx}
-Eta
introduce
\beta\eta
-equivalence
for rewrites. Note that
\mathrel{\approx}
-BetaTR
and
\mathrel{\approx}
-BetaRT

restrict either the body of the abstraction or the argument to a unit rewrite,
thus avoiding the issue mentioned in the introduction where a naive combination
of composition and
\beta\eta
-equivalence can lead to invalid rewrites.
Permutation equivalent rewrites are coinitial and cofinal modulo
=_{\beta\eta}
, a property which may easily be proved by induction on the derivation of
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma
:

Lemma 13
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p_{1}}:\sigma

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}q_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}q_{1}}:\sigma

be such that
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma
.
Then
\rho^{\mathsf{src}}=_{\beta\eta}\sigma^{\mathsf{src}}

and
\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}=_{\beta\eta}\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}}
.

Note that there are no explicit sequencing equations such as the I/O equations
3
3

3

I:\varrho(\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{n})\mathrel{\approx}l(\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{n})\cdot\varrho(t_{1},...,t_{n})
and
O:\varrho(\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{n})\mathrel{\approx}\varrho(s_{1},...,s_{n})\cdot r(\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{n})

defining permutation equivalence in the first-order case
[
Ter03
]
and the corresponding equations flat-l and flat-r of
[
Bru08
]
for the higher-order case. Nonetheless, we can derive the following
coherence equation whose proof is by induction on the derivation of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p_{1}}:B
and which will motivate our upcoming notion of substitution of rewrites (Definition
4.1
):

Lemma 14
.

Let

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p_{1}}:B

and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}q_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}q_{1}}:A
.
Then:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}q_{0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}p_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}&\mathrel{\approx}&p_{0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}q_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}&(\textsf{{$\mathrel{\approx}$-Perm}})\end{array}

Graphically, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}
\scriptstyle{s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}}
\textstyle{t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}}
\textstyle{s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}
\textstyle{t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}

{exa}

Consider the two following reduction sequences,
where (
R_{2}
) is the same as in Example
2
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}R_{1}&:&\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x)\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x)\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{g}\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x))\\
R_{2}&:&\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x)\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{f}\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x))\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{f}\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x))\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{g}\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x))\end{array}

Reduction sequence
R_{1}
can be encoded as the rewrite
\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\vartheta\,x);\varrho\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x)
and
R_{2}
as
\varrho\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathbf{f}\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\vartheta\,x))\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\vartheta\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x))
. These two rewrites are permutation equivalent:

\begin{array}[]{lllll}&\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\vartheta\,x)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\varrho\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x)\\
\approx^{(\footnotesize{\textsf{{Eta}}})}&\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\varrho\,\mathbf{g}\\
=&(\mathbf{mu}\,y){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}y{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\varrho\,y){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}y{\backslash}\mathbf{g}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\\
\approx^{(\footnotesize{\textsf{{Perm}}})}&(\varrho\,y){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}y{\backslash}\mathbf{f}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(y\,(\mathbf{mu}\,y)){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}y{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\vartheta{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\\
=&\varrho\,\mathbf{f}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\vartheta\,(\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta)\\
\approx^{(\footnotesize{\textsf{{IdL}}})}&\varrho\,\mathbf{f}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\mathbf{f}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\vartheta)\,(\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta)\\
\approx^{(\footnotesize{\textsf{{IdR}}})}&\varrho\,\mathbf{f}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\mathbf{f}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\vartheta)\,((\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\mathbf{mu}\,\mathbf{g}))\\
\approx^{(\footnotesize{\textsf{{App}}})}&\varrho\,\mathbf{f}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathbf{f}\,(\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\vartheta\,(\mathbf{mu}\,\mathbf{g})\\
\approx^{(\footnotesize{\textsf{{Eta}}})}&\varrho\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathbf{f}\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\vartheta\,x))\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\vartheta\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x))\end{array}

4.1.
Substitution of Rewrites in Rewrites.

The
\mathrel{\approx}
-Perm
rule of Lemma
14

motivates the following definition of substitution of a rewrite inside another rewrite:

{defi}
[Rewrite/Rewrite Substitution]

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:B

and let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t^{\prime}}:A
. Then

\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.
Note that, by the
\mathrel{\approx}
-Perm
rule (Lemma
14
),

\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\approx}s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}
,
which could be taken as an alternative definition.
Graphically:

\textstyle{s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}
\scriptstyle{s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}}
\textstyle{t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}}
\textstyle{s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}
\textstyle{t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}}

One subtle remark is that
\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}

depends on
t
and
s^{\prime}
,
and hence on the particular typing derivations
for
\rho
and
\sigma
.
Lemma
16
(3) below
ensures that the value of
\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}

does not depend, up to permutation equivalence, on those typing
derivations.
It relies on a number of preliminary results,
all of which express that equivalence.
The proof of Lemma
15
(1) is by induction on the derivation of
\rho

and that of Lemma
15
(2) by induction on the derivation of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}:B
.

Lemma 15

(Congruence of term equivalence below substitution)
.

1.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:B

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r^{\prime}}:A
.
Then
\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\approx}\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

2.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r}=_{\beta\eta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r^{\prime}}:B

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A
.
Then
r{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\approx}r^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

Lemma 16

(Congruence of rewrite equivalence below substitution)
.

1.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:B

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t^{\prime}}:B

be such that
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\rho^{\prime}
,
and let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r}:A
.
Then
\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\approx}\rho^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

2.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r}:B

and let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t^{\prime}}:A

such that
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\rho^{\prime}
.
Then
r{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\approx}r{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

3.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:B

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t^{\prime}}:B

be such that
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\rho^{\prime}
,
and
let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p}:A

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p^{\prime}}:A

be such that
\sigma\mathrel{\approx}\sigma^{\prime}
.
Then

\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\approx}\rho^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

Proof 4.1
.

The first item is by induction on the derivation of
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\rho^{\prime}
and the second on that of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r}:B
. For the third we reason as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}&=&\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\\
&\mathrel{\approx}&\rho^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}&\text{by Lemma~\ref{lemma:congruence_permeq_subs}(1)}\\
&\mathrel{\approx}&\rho^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}&\text{by Lemma~\ref{lemma:congruence_termeq_subs}(1)}\\
&\mathrel{\approx}&\rho^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}t^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}&\text{by Lemma~\ref{lemma:congruence_termeq_subs}(2)}\\
&\mathrel{\approx}&\rho^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}r^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}t^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}&\text{by Lemma~\ref{lemma:congruence_permeq_subs}(2)}\\
&=&\rho^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}\end{array}

Rewrite/rewrite substitution generalizes
rewrite/term and term/rewrite substitution, in the sense that

\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\approx}\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\underline{t}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}

and
s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\approx}\underline{s}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

Other important facts involving rewrite/rewrite substitution
are the following.
First, it commutes with abstraction, application, and composition:

Lemma 17
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}q_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}q_{1}}:A
.
The following recursive equations hold for rewrite/rewrite substitution:

1.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A,y:B}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}:C
, then

(\lambda y.\rho){\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\approx}\lambda y.\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}y{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

2.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho_{1}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}:B\to C

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho_{2}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t_{1}}:B
,
then

(\rho_{1}\,\rho_{2}){\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\approx}\rho_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}\,\rho_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

3.

Suppose that:

•

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho_{1}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}:B

and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho_{2}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{2}}:B

•

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma_{1}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t_{1}}:B

and
