{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma_{2}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t_{1}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t_{2}}:B

Then

(\rho_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho_{2}){\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\approx}\rho_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

Proof 4.2
.

We check each item separately:

1.

Abstraction:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}(\lambda y.\rho){\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}&=&(\lambda y.\rho){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}q_{0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\lambda y.s_{1}){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\\
&=&(\lambda y.\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}q_{0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}})\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\lambda y.s_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}})\\
&\mathrel{\approx}&\lambda y.(\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}q_{0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}s_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}})&\text{by {{$\mathrel{\approx}$-Abs}}}\\
&=&\lambda y.\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}\end{array}

2.

Application:
similar to the previous case, using the
\mathrel{\approx}
-App
rule.

3.

Composition:
We work implicitly modulo associativity of composition (“
\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}
”),
using the
\mathrel{\approx}
-Assoc
rule:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}(\rho_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho_{2}){\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}&=&(\rho_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho_{2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t_{0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}s_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\\
&=&\rho_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t_{0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t_{0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}s_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\\
&\mathrel{\approx}&\rho_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t_{0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t_{0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}s_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}s_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}&\text{by ($\star$)}\\
&\mathrel{\approx}&\rho_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t_{0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}s_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}s_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}&\text{by Lemma~\ref{lemma:coherence}}\\
&\mathrel{\approx}&\rho_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}\end{array}

The
(\star)
is the following property which can be proved by induction on the derivation of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:B
:
Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}:B

and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p_{1}}:A

and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p_{1}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p_{2}}:A
.
Then:

s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\approx}s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

Second, an analog of the substitution lemma holds:

Proposition 18
.

Suppose that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A,y:B}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}:C
,

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,y:B}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t_{1}}:A
, and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\tau}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r_{1}}:B
.
Then

\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}y{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\tau{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\approx}\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}y{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\tau{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}y{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\tau{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

Finally, as discussed above, a
\beta
-rule for arbitrary rewrites
holds in the following form:

Lemma 19

(Rewrite/rewrite
\beta
-reduction rule)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s_{1}}:B

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t_{1}}:A
.
Then the following equivalence, called
\mathrel{\approx}
-BetaRR
, holds:

(\lambda x.\rho)\,\sigma\mathrel{\approx}\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt(\textsf{{$\mathrel{\approx}$-BetaRR}})

Proof 4.3
.

\begin{array}[]{rcll}(\lambda x.\rho)\,\sigma&\mathrel{\approx}&((\lambda x.\rho)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\lambda x.s_{1}))\,\sigma&\textsf{{$\mathrel{\approx}$-IdR}}\\
&\mathrel{\approx}&((\lambda x.\rho)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\lambda x.s_{1}))\,(t_{0}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma)&\textsf{{$\mathrel{\approx}$-IdL}}\\
&\mathrel{\approx}&(\lambda x.\rho)\,t_{0}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\lambda x.s_{1})\,\sigma&\textsf{{$\mathrel{\approx}$-App}}\\
&\mathrel{\approx}&\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t_{0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\lambda x.s_{1})\,\sigma&\textsf{{$\mathrel{\approx}$-BetaRT}}\\
&\mathrel{\approx}&\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}t_{0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}s_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}&\textsf{{$\mathrel{\approx}$-BetaTR}}\\
&=&\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}\end{array}

A summary of the three notions of substitution introduced so far is as follows:

\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}

rewrite/term

Standard notion of (capture-avoiding) substitution

s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}

term/rewrite

Definition
3.1

\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\sigma{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}

rewrite/rewrite

Definition
4.1

5.
Flattening

Allowing composition to be nested within
application and abstraction can give rise to rewrites in which it
is not obvious what reduction sequences of steps are being denoted.
An example from the previous
section might be the rewrite
((\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\vartheta)\,((\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\vartheta\,x))\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x)))
which denotes a reduction
sequence
\mathbf{f}\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x))\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{g}\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x))
that replaces both
occurrences of
\mathbf{f}
with
\mathbf{g}
simultaneously. This section
shows how rewrites can be “flattened” so as to expose an underlying reduction
sequence, expressed as a canonical or
flat
rewrite.

Specifically, we define an operation called
flattening

that given an arbitrary rewrite
\rho
computes a permutation equivalent
flat rewrite
\rho^{\mathcal{F}}
.
Flat rewrites do not allow the composition operator “
\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}
”
to appear inside
\lambda
-abstractions
nor applications, so for instance
(\varrho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\vartheta)\,\mathbf{c}
is not flat,
whereas the permutation equivalent rewrite
\varrho\,\mathbf{c}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\vartheta\,\mathbf{c}

is flat.
Flattening is a way of ensuring
that arbitrary rewrites can always be converted into an equivalent
higher-order proof-term in Bruggink’s
[
Bru08
]
sense,
given that, as mentioned in the introduction, Bruggink disallows compositions
to appear inside
\lambda
-abstractions and applications.
In addition, we define an equivalence relation
\rho\mathrel{\sim}\sigma

between
flat
rewrites, and we show that in general

(\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma)\iff(\rho^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{\sim}\sigma^{\mathcal{F}})
.
This means that flattening can be understood as a
semantics
for rewrites.
One additional use of flattening will be to show that permutation equivalence is decidable (
cf.
end of Section
6
).

5.1.
The Flattening Rewrite System

A
multistep
(
\mu,\nu,\xi,\ldots
) is a rewrite without any occurrences of the composition operator.
The capture-avoiding substitution
of the free occurrences of
x
in
\mu
by
\nu

is written
\mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\{}}}x\backslash\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\}}}}
,
which is in turn a multistep.

{defi}
[Flat Multistep and Flat Rewrite]

A
flat multistep
(
\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu},\ldots
), is a multistep
in
\beta
-normal form,
i.e.
without subterms of the form
(\lambda x.\mu)\,\nu
.
A
flat rewrite
(
\hat{\rho},\hat{\sigma},\ldots
), is a rewrite
given by the grammar

\hat{\rho}::=\hat{\mu}\,\mid\,\hat{\rho}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\hat{\sigma}
.

Flat rewrites use the composition operator “
\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}
” at the top level,
that is
they are of the form
\hat{\mu}_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\ldots\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\hat{\mu}_{n}

(up to associativity of “
\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}
”), where each
\hat{\mu}_{i}

is a flat multistep.
Note that we do not require the
\hat{\mu}_{i}

to be in
\eta
-normal form nor in
\overline{\eta}
-normal form.
As mentioned in the introduction, flattening is achieved by means of a

rewriting system whose objects are themselves rewrites
(Definition
5.1
)
which is shown to be
confluent and terminating
(Proposition
30
).

We also formulate an equational theory
defining a relation
\rho\mathrel{\sim}\sigma

of
flat permutation equivalence

between flat rewrites (Definition
5.5
).
The main result of this section is that
permutation equivalence is
sound and complete

with respect
to flat permutation equivalence (Theorem
36
).

Remark 20
.

A substitution
\mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\{}}}x\backslash\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\}}}}

in which
\mu
is a term is a term/rewrite substitution,
i.e.
s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\{}}}x\backslash\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\}}}}=s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.
A substitution
in which
\nu
is a term is a rewrite/term substitution,
i.e.
\mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\{}}}x\backslash s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\}}}}=\mu{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

{defi}

[Flattening Rewrite System
\mathcal{F}
]

The flattening system
\mathcal{F}
is given by the
following rules, closed under arbitrary contexts,
defined between
typable
rewrites:

\begin{array}[]{rclrl}\lambda x.(\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma)&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}&(\lambda x.\rho)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\lambda x.\sigma)&&\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-Abs}}\\
(\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma)\,\mu&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}&(\rho\,\underline{\mu^{\mathsf{src}}})\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\sigma\,\mu)&&\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-App1}}\\
\mu\,(\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma)&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}&(\mu\,\rho)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\underline{\mu^{\mathsf{tgt}}}\,\sigma)&&\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-App2}}\\
(\rho_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho_{2})\,(\sigma_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma_{2})&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}&((\rho_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho_{2})\,\underline{\sigma_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}})\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\underline{\rho_{2}^{\mathsf{tgt}}}\,(\sigma_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma_{2}))&&\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-App3}}\\
(\lambda x.\mu)\,\nu&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}&\mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\{}}}x\backslash\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\}}}}&&\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-BetaM}}\\
\lambda x.\mu\,x&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}&\mu&\text{if $x\notin\mathsf{fv}(\mu)$}&\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-EtaM}}\end{array}

Note that rules
\mathcal{F}
-BetaM
and
\mathcal{F}
-EtaM
apply to multisteps only.
The reduction relation
\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}
is the union of all these rules,
closed by compatibility under arbitrary contexts.
We write
\rho^{\mathcal{F}}
for the unique
\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}
-normal form of
\rho
. Uniqueness will be proved below (Proposition
30
).

{exa}

Consider a rewriting rule

\varrho:\mathbf{c}\rightarrowtriangle\mathbf{d}:A
.
The rewrite
(\lambda x.(x\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}x))\,\varrho

can be flattened as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}(\lambda x.(x\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}x))\,\varrho&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-Abs}}}&((\lambda x.x)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\lambda x.x))\,\varrho\\
&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-App1}}}&(\lambda x.x)\,\mathbf{c}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\lambda x.x)\,\varrho\\
&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-BetaM}}}&\mathbf{c}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\lambda x.x)\,\varrho\\
&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-BetaM}}}&\mathbf{c}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\varrho\end{array}

5.2.
Basic Properties of the Flattening Rewrite System

We present some basic properties of
\mathcal{F}
. First a remark. Flattening does not alter the source or target of a rewrite, modulo
\beta\eta
-equality. Moreover, in the particular case that an applied flattening rule is one not in the set
\{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-BetaM}},\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-EtaM}}\}
, then the source and target are literally the same.

Remark 21
.

If
\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\mathtt{x}}\sigma

and
\mathtt{x}\notin\{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-BetaM}},\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-EtaM}}\}

then
\rho^{\mathsf{src}}=\sigma^{\mathsf{src}}

and
\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}=\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}}
.

Next we consider flattening below term/rewrite and rewrite/term
substitution (Lemma
22
). The proof the first item is
straightforward by induction on
s
and that of the second by induction on
the context under which the step
\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}\rho^{\prime}
takes place.

Lemma 22
.

Suppose
\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}\rho^{\prime}
. Then:

1.

s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

2.

\rho{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}\rho^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\}}}}
.

Similarly, we have the following proved by induction on the target judgment:

Lemma 23
.

If
\mathtt{x}\in\{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-BetaM}},\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-EtaM}}\}
then:

•

\mu\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\mathtt{x}}\mu^{\prime}
implies

\mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\{}}}x\backslash\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\mathtt{x}}\mu^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\{}}}x\backslash\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\}}}}

•

\nu\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\mathtt{x}}\nu^{\prime}
implies

\mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\{}}}x\backslash\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}_{\mathtt{x}}}\mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\{}}}x\backslash\nu^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\}}}}
.

The following result states that
\mathcal{F}
-BetaM
and
\mathcal{F}
-EtaM
steps can be postponed
after steps of other kinds. We will make use of it in our proof of SN of flattening. The proof is by induction on the context under which the step

\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\mathtt{x}}\sigma
takes place.

Lemma 24
.

Let
\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\mathtt{x}}\sigma\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\mathtt{y}}\tau

where
\mathtt{x}\in\{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-BetaM}},\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-EtaM}}\}

and
\mathtt{y}\notin\{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-BetaM}},\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-EtaM}}\}
.
Then there is a rewrite
\upsilon

such that
\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\mathtt{y}}\upsilon\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\mathtt{x}}^{+}\tau
.

The following auxiliary result will be of use in proving confluence of
\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}
; its proof is by induction on
\rho
. Here
\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{=}_{\mathtt{x}}}
denotes
the
reflexive
closure of
\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\mathtt{x}}
:

Lemma 25

(Source-Target of Beta/Eta Flattening)
.

If
\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\mathtt{x}}\sigma

where
\mathtt{x}\in\{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-BetaM}},\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-EtaM}}\}
,
then
\underline{\rho^{\mathsf{src}}}\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{=}_{\mathtt{x}}}\underline{\sigma^{\mathsf{src}}}

and
\underline{\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}}\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{=}_{\mathtt{x}}}\underline{\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}}}
.

We next address strong normalization (Proposition
28
) and confluence (Proposition
29
).

5.3.
Strong Normalization of the Flattening Rewrite System

Since the union of
\mathcal{F}
-BetaM
and
\mathcal{F}
-EtaM
can
easily be seen to be SN (given that they essentially operate on
simply typed terms) and in view of Lemma
24
, in
order to obtain SN of
\mathcal{F}
we are left with the task
of proving that the union of all the remaining rules is SN. This can be done by defining an appropriate well-founded measure
on rewrites. Consider

\#(\rho)\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,(\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\rho),\#_{\mathsf{w}}(\rho))
where:

{defi}

[Heavy applications]
An application
\rho\,\sigma
is
heavy

if
\rho
and
\sigma
are not multisteps,

i.e.
if both
\rho
and
\sigma
contain compositions (“
\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}
”).
We write
\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\rho)
to stand for the number of heavy
applications in
\rho
. More precisely:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\#_{\mathsf{h}}(x)=\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\mathbf{c})=\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\varrho)&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&0\\
\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\lambda x.\rho)&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\rho)\\
\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\rho\,\sigma)&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\rho)+\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\sigma)+\begin{cases}1&\text{if $\rho\,\sigma$ is heavy}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\\
\end{cases}\\
\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma)&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\rho)+\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\sigma)\end{array}

{defi}

[Weight of a Rewrite]
The
weight
of a rewrite
\rho

is a non-negative integer
\#_{\mathsf{w}}(\rho)

defined inductively as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\#_{\mathsf{w}}(x)=\#_{\mathsf{w}}(\mathbf{c})=\#_{\mathsf{w}}(\varrho)&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&0\\
\#_{\mathsf{w}}(\lambda x.\rho)&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&2\,\#_{\mathsf{w}}(\rho)\\
\#_{\mathsf{w}}(\rho\,\sigma)&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&2\,\#_{\mathsf{w}}(\rho)+2\,\#_{\mathsf{w}}(\sigma)\\
\#_{\mathsf{w}}(\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma)&\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}&1+\#_{\mathsf{w}}(\rho)+\#_{\mathsf{w}}(\sigma)\end{array}

We have the following two results, both of which are proved by induction on the context under which the
step
\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\mathtt{x}}\sigma
takes place:

Lemma 26

(Decrease of heavy applications)
.

Let
\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\mathtt{x}}\sigma

where
\mathtt{x}\notin\{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-BetaM}},\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-EtaM}}\}
.
Then
\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\rho)\geq\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\sigma)
.
Furthermore if
\mathtt{x}=\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-App3}}

then
\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\rho)>\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\sigma)
.

Lemma 27

(Decrease of weight)
.

Let
\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\mathtt{x}}\sigma

where
\mathtt{x}\in\{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-Abs}},\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-App1}},\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-App2}}\}
.
Then
\#_{\mathsf{w}}(\rho)>\#_{\mathsf{w}}(\sigma)
.

This suffices to show that
\#(\bullet)
is well-founded.
A sketch of the entire argument of the proof of SN for
\mathcal{F}
is presented below.

Proposition 28
.

The flattening system
\mathcal{F}
is strongly normalizing.

Proof 5.1
.

Recall that
\mathcal{F}
-BetaM
and
\mathcal{F}
-EtaM
steps can be postponed
after steps of other kinds (Lemma
24
).
Hence, by standard rewriting techniques,
SN of
\mathcal{F}

can be reduced
to SN of
\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-BetaM}}}\cup\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-EtaM}}}

on one hand,
plus SN of
\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-Abs}}}\cup\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-App1}}}\cup\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-App2}}}\cup\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-App3}}}

on the other one.

It is immediate to show that the union of
\mathcal{F}
-BetaM
and
\mathcal{F}
-EtaM

is SN, given that (typable) multisteps can be understood as
simply typed
\lambda
-terms,
by regarding constants (
\mathbf{c},\mathbf{d},\ldots
)
and rule symbols (
\varrho,\vartheta,\ldots
)
as free variables of their corresponding types.
Hence termination of
\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-BetaM}}}\cup\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-EtaM}}}

is reduced to termination of
\beta\eta
-reduction in the simply-typed

\lambda
-calculus.

To show that the system without
\mathcal{F}
-BetaM
and
\mathcal{F}
-EtaM

is SN, consider the measure on rewrites
given by
\#(\rho)\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}}\,(\#_{\mathsf{h}}(\rho),\#_{\mathsf{w}}(\rho))

with the lexicographic order.
It is then easy to show that
if
\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}_{\mathtt{x}}\sigma

with
\mathtt{x}\notin\{\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-BetaM}},\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-EtaM}}\}

then
\#(\rho)>\#(\sigma)
.
Indeed, by Lemma
26

we know that
\mathcal{F}
-App3
steps strictly decrease the first component
and other kinds of steps do not increase it.
Moreover, by Lemma
27
,
we know that
\mathcal{F}
-Abs
,
\mathcal{F}
-App1
, and
\mathcal{F}
-App2

steps strictly decrease the second component.

5.4.
Confluence of the Flattening Rewrite System

Confluence of
\mathcal{F}
follows from Newman’s lemma.

Proposition 29
.

The flattening system
\mathcal{F}
is confluent.

Proof 5.2
.

By Newman’s lemma, given that
\mathcal{F}
is SN (Proposition
28
),
it suffices to show that it is weakly Church–Rosser (WCR).
Let
\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}\rho_{1}
and
\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}\rho_{2}
. One shows that there exists a rewrite
\rho_{3}
such
that
\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}\rho_{3}
and
\rho_{1}\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}\rho_{3}
.
The proof is by induction on
\rho
.

Proposition 30
.

The flattening system
\mathcal{F}

is strongly normalizing and confluent.

5.5.
Flat Permutation Equivalence.

We now turn to the definition of the relation
\rho\mathrel{\sim}\sigma

of flat permutation equivalence.
The key notion to define is the following ternary relation:

{defi}

[Splitting]
Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\mu}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\mu_{1}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r_{1}}:A

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\mu_{2}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r_{2}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t^{\prime}}:A

be multisteps.
We say that
\mu

splits
into
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}

if the following inductively defined ternary relation,
written
\mu\Leftrightarrow\mu_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mu_{2}
, holds:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 17.94783pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=35.89566pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{SVar}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x\Leftrightarrow x\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}x$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 14.26384pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=28.52768pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{SCon}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbf{c}\Leftrightarrow\mathbf{c}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathbf{c}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 17.04509pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=34.09018pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{SRuleL}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\varrho\Leftrightarrow\varrho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\underline{\varrho^{\mathsf{tgt}}}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 15.65623pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=31.31247pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{SRuleR}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\varrho\Leftrightarrow\underline{\varrho^{\mathsf{src}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\varrho$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 23.98956pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\mu\Leftrightarrow\mu_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mu_{2}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=93.7777pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{SAbs}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lambda x.\mu\Leftrightarrow\lambda x.\mu_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\lambda x.\mu_{2}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\mu\Leftrightarrow\mu_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mu_{2}\hskip 14.22636pt\nu\Leftrightarrow\nu_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\nu_{2}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=102.56651pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{SApp}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 13.98817pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\,\nu\Leftrightarrow\mu_{1}\,\nu_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mu_{2}\,\nu_{2}$}}}}\end{array}

{exa}

Consider the HRS of Example
3
. It has the following two rewriting rules:

\langle\varrho,\mathbf{mu}(\lambda y.x\,y),x\,(\mathbf{mu}(\lambda y.x\,y))\rangle

and

\langle\vartheta,\mathbf{f}\,x,\mathbf{g}\,x\rangle
. Then
\varrho\,\vartheta
splits into
(\lambda x.\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda y.x\,y))\,\vartheta
and
\varrho\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x)
:

\varrho\,\vartheta\Leftrightarrow(\lambda x.\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda y.x\,y))\,\vartheta\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\varrho\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x)

Note that
\varrho\,\vartheta\Leftrightarrow(\lambda x.\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda y.x\,y))\,\vartheta\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\varrho\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x)
follows from
SApp
,
SRuleR
for the upper left hypothesis and
SRuleL
for the upper right one.

Also,
\vartheta\,(\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta)
splits into
(\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x)\,(\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta)
and
\vartheta\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x))
:

\vartheta\,(\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta)\Leftrightarrow(\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x)\,(\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\vartheta\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x))

{defi}

[Flat permutation equivalence]

Flat permutation equivalence judgments are of the form:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash({\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t})\mathrel{\sim}({\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}{\rho}^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t^{\prime}}):A
,
meaning that
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}

are equivalent rewrites,
with sources
s
and
s^{\prime}
respectively,
and targets
t
and
t^{\prime}
respectively.
The rewrites
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
are

assumed to be in
\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}
-normal form
,
which in particular means that they must be flat rewrites (Definition
5.1
).
Sometimes we write
\rho\mathrel{\sim}\rho^{\prime}

if
\Gamma,s,t,s^{\prime},t^{\prime},A
are irrelevant
or clear from the context.
Derivability is defined by
the two following axioms,
which are closed by
reflexivity, symmetry, transitivity, and closure under

composition contexts

(given by

\mathtt{S}::=\Box\mid\mathtt{S}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho\mid\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathtt{S}
):

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }c@{\ }lrl}(\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tau&\mathrel{\sim}&\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\sigma\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tau)&&\textsf{{$\mathrel{\sim}$-Assoc}}\\
\mu&\mathrel{\sim}&\mu_{1}^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mu_{2}^{\mathcal{F}}&\text{if $\mu\Leftrightarrow\mu_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mu_{2}$}&\textsf{{$\mathrel{\sim}$-Perm}}\end{array}

Note that in
\mathrel{\sim}
-Perm
,
-^{\mathcal{F}}
operates over multisteps. So the only rules of
\mathcal{F}
that are applied here are the
\mathcal{F}
-BetaM
and
\mathcal{F}
-EtaM
rules.

{exa}

With the same notation as in Example
4
,
it can be checked that the rewrites

\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\vartheta\,x)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\varrho\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x)

and

\varrho\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathbf{f}\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\vartheta\,x))\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\vartheta\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x))

are permutation equivalent by means of flattening.
Indeed, using the
\mathrel{\sim}
-Perm
rule three times:

\begin{array}[]{lll@{\ }l}\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\varrho\,\mathbf{g}&\mathrel{\sim}&\varrho\,\vartheta&\text{Example~\ref{example:splitting}}\\
&\mathrel{\sim}&\varrho\,\mathbf{f}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\vartheta\,(\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta)&\\
&\mathrel{\sim}&\varrho\,\mathbf{f}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\vartheta(\mathbf{\mu}\,\mathbf{g}))&\text{Example~\ref{example:splitting}}\end{array}

Hence:

\begin{array}[]{lll}(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\vartheta\,x)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\varrho\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x))^{\mathcal{F}}&=&\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\varrho\,\mathbf{g}\\
&\mathrel{\sim}&\varrho\,\mathbf{f}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\vartheta(\mathbf{\mu}\,\mathbf{g}))\\
&=&(\varrho\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathbf{f}\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\vartheta\,x))\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\vartheta\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{g}\,x)))^{\mathcal{F}}\end{array}

We next address soundness and completeness of
\mathrel{\sim}
with respect to
\mathrel{\approx}
.

5.6.
Soundness of Flat Permutation Equivalence

Soundness of flat permutation equivalence, namely that if
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t^{\prime}}:A
, then
\rho^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{\sim}\sigma^{\mathcal{F}}
implies
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma
, is stated as Proposition
34
. It follows from the fact that reduction
\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}
in the flattening system
\mathcal{F}

is included in permutation equivalence (
\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}\sigma
implies
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma
, Lemma
31
)
and, similarly, flat permutation equivalence
is included in permutation equivalence (
\rho\mathrel{\sim}\sigma
implies
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma
, Lemma
33
).

Lemma 31
.

If
\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}\sigma
then
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma
.

Proof 5.3
.

It suffices to show that all the axioms of
the flattening system
\mathcal{F}

relate permutation equivalent rewrites. We present two sample cases:

1.

\mathcal{F}
-App3
:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}(\rho_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho_{2})(\sigma_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma_{2})&\mathrel{\approx}&((\rho_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho_{2})\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\underline{\rho_{2}^{\mathsf{tgt}}})(\underline{\sigma_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\sigma_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma_{2}))&\text{by {{$\mathrel{\approx}$-IdL}} and {{$\mathrel{\approx}$-IdR}}}\\
&\mathrel{\approx}&((\rho_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho_{2})\,\underline{\sigma_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}})\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\underline{\rho_{2}^{\mathsf{tgt}}}\,(\sigma_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma_{2}))&\text{by {{$\mathrel{\approx}$-App}}}\end{array}

2.

\mathcal{F}
-BetaM
:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}(\lambda x.\mu)\,\nu&\mathrel{\approx}&\mu{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\nu{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}&\text{by {{$\mathrel{\approx}$-BetaRR}}~(Lemma~\ref{lemma:betaRR})}\\
&\mathrel{\approx}&\mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\{}}}x\backslash\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\}}}}&\end{array}

The last step follows from the property that
\mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\{}}}x\backslash\nu{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\}}}}\mathrel{\approx}\mu{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\nu{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\bm{\}}}}
, which is proved by
induction on the derivation of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\mu}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p_{0}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}p_{1}}:B
.

Lemma 32
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\mu}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\mu_{1}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r_{1}}:A

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\mu_{2}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r_{2}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t^{\prime}}:A

be such that
\mu\Leftrightarrow\mu_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mu_{2}
.
Then
\mu\mathrel{\approx}\mu_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mu_{2}

Proof 5.4
.

By induction on the derivation of
\mu\Leftrightarrow\mu_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mu_{2}
using
\mathrel{\approx}
-IdL
,
\mathrel{\approx}
-IdR
,
\mathrel{\approx}
-Abs
,
\mathrel{\approx}
-App
and the IH.

Lemma 33
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t^{\prime}}:A

be such that
\rho\mathrel{\sim}\sigma
.
Then
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma
.

Proof 5.5
.

By induction on the derivation of
\rho\mathrel{\sim}\sigma
.
Reflexivity, transitivity, symmetry, and closure under composition
contexts is immediate.
The interesting case is when an axiom is applied at the root:

1.

\mathrel{\sim}
-Assoc
:
Let
(\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tau\mathrel{\sim}\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\sigma\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tau)
.
Then by
\mathrel{\approx}
-Assoc

also
(\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tau\mathrel{\approx}\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\sigma\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tau)
.

2.

\mathrel{\sim}
-Perm
:
Let
\mu\mathrel{\sim}\mu_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mu_{2}
where

\mu\Leftrightarrow\mu_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mu_{2}
.
Then by Lemma
32

we have that
\mu\mathrel{\approx}\mu_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mu_{2}
.

We thus obtain soundness of flat permutation equivalence as an immediate consequence of Lemma
31
and Lemma
33
:

Proposition 34

(Soundness of flat permutation equivalence)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t^{\prime}}:A
.
Then
\rho^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{\sim}\sigma^{\mathcal{F}}
implies
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma
.

5.7.
Completeness

Completeness, namely that
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma

implies
\rho^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{\sim}\sigma^{\mathcal{F}}
, and stated
as Proposition
35
below, requires considerably more work.
We begin by presenting some auxiliary notions and results.
First a shorthand:

\rho^{\blacktriangleleft}
and
\rho^{\blacktriangleright}

denote the
\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}
-normal forms of the source and target, respectively,
that is,
(\underline{\rho^{\mathsf{src}}})^{\mathcal{F}}

and
(\underline{\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}})^{\mathcal{F}}
.
We begin by introducing certain contexts called
composition trees
:

\mathtt{K}::=\Box\mid\mathtt{K}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathtt{K}

For each
n\geq 1
, an
n
-hole composition tree

is a composition tree with
n
occurrences of the
hole

\Box
.
If
\mathtt{K}
is an
n
-hole composition tree,
we write
\mathtt{K}\langle\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{n}\rangle
to stand for the
rewrite that results from replacing the
i
-th hole of
\mathtt{K}
for
\rho_{i}

for each
1\leq i\leq n
.
For example
((\Box\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\Box)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\Box)
is a 3-hole
composition tree and

((\Box\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\Box)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\Box)\langle\rho,\sigma,\tau\rangle=(\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma)\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tau
. Composition trees give us ready access to all the multisteps in a flat rewrite. We next list some useful properties on flat rewrites, presented in terms of compositions trees.

P1:

Rules
\mathcal{F}
-Abs
,
\mathcal{F}
-App1
,
\mathcal{F}
-App2
, and
\mathcal{F}
-App3
of Definition
5.1
can be generalized to composition trees.

1.:

Generalized
\mathcal{F}
-Abs
.

\lambda x.\mathtt{K}\langle\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{n}\rangle\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}\mathtt{K}\langle\lambda x.\rho_{1},\ldots,\lambda x.\rho_{n}\rangle
.

2.:

Generalized
\mathcal{F}
-App1
.

\mathtt{K}\langle\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{n-1},\rho_{n}\rangle\,\mu\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}\mathtt{K}\langle(\rho_{1}\,\underline{\mu^{\mathsf{src}}}),\ldots,(\rho_{n-1}\,\underline{\mu^{\mathsf{src}}}),(\rho_{n}\,\mu)\rangle
.

3.:

Generalized
\mathcal{F}
-App2
.

\mu\,\mathtt{K}\langle\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\ldots,\rho_{n}\rangle\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}\mathtt{K}\langle(\mu\,\rho_{1}),(\underline{\mu^{\mathsf{tgt}}}\,\rho_{2}),\ldots,(\underline{\mu^{\mathsf{tgt}}}\,\rho_{n})\rangle
.

4.:

Generalized
\mathcal{F}
-App3
.

If
n,m>1
then:

\mathtt{K}\langle\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{n}\rangle\,\mathtt{K}^{\prime}\langle\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m}\rangle\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}\mathtt{K}\langle(\rho_{1}\,\underline{\sigma_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}}),\ldots,(\rho_{n}\,\underline{\sigma_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}})\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathtt{K}^{\prime}\langle(\underline{\rho_{n}^{\mathsf{tgt}}}\,\sigma_{1}),\ldots,(\underline{\rho_{n}^{\mathsf{tgt}}}\,\sigma_{m})\rangle

P2:

Congruence below abstraction and application also generalize to composition trees, obtaining
generalized congruence equations
in following sense:

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle(\lambda x.\mu_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\lambda x.\mu_{n})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{\sim}\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle(\lambda x.\nu_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\lambda x.\nu_{m})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\\
\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle(\mu_{1}\,\underline{s})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\mu_{n}\,\underline{s})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{\sim}\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle(\nu_{1}\,\underline{s})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\nu_{m}\,\underline{s})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\\
\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle(\underline{s}\,\mu_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\underline{s}\,\mu_{n})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{\sim}\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle(\underline{s}\,\nu_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\underline{s}\,\nu_{m})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\end{array}

where

\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}\rangle\mathrel{\sim}\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{m}\rangle
and let
s
be an arbitrary term.
Note that the multisteps
\mu_{i}
and
\nu_{i}
are in

\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}
-normal form because the
\mathrel{\sim}
relation only relates
flat rewrites.
But observe that
\lambda x.\mu_{i}
and
\lambda x.\nu_{i}

may not necessarily be in
\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}
-normal form because
there may be an
\mathcal{F}
-EtaM
redex at the root. Thus the proof of the first item requires some care.

P3:

Flattening of an application of two flat rewrites expressed in terms of their composition trees.
Let
\rho=\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}\rangle

and
\sigma=\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{m}\rangle

be flat rewrites.
Then

\rho\,\sigma\ \mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}\mathrel{\sim}\ \mathtt{K}_{1}\langle(\mu_{1}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\mu_{n}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle(\mu_{n}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\nu_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\mu_{n}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\nu_{m})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle

P4:

Equivalence for term/rewrite substitution of a composition.
Let
s
be a term,
let
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}
arbitrary multisteps,
and let
\mathtt{K}
a composition tree.
Then:

s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\mathtt{K}\langle\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\,\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}\mathrel{\sim}\,\mathtt{K}\langle s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\mu_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}},\ldots,s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\mu_{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\rangle^{\mathcal{F}}

Proposition 35

(Completeness of flat permutation equivalence)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t^{\prime}}:A
.
Then
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma
implies
\rho^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{\sim}\sigma^{\mathcal{F}}
.

Proof 5.6
.

By induction on the derivation of
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma
.
In the proof, sometimes we implicitly use the fact that
\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}
is
strongly normalizing (Proposition
28
)
and confluent (Proposition
29
).
In particular, note that

(\rho\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma)^{\mathcal{F}}=\rho^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma^{\mathcal{F}}

and more in general

\mathtt{K}\langle\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{n}\rangle^{\mathcal{F}}=\mathtt{K}\langle\rho_{1}^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,\rho_{n}^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle
.
In the inductive proof, the cases for reflexivity,
symmetry, and transitivity are immediate. We analyze the cases when
a rule is applied at the root, as well as congruence closure under
rewrite constructors. Root cases
\mathrel{\approx}
-Assoc
and
\mathrel{\approx}
-Abs
are straightforward. Root case
\mathrel{\approx}
-IdR
is similar to
\mathrel{\approx}
-IdL
, so among the two we only consider the latter. Likewise, the root case
\mathrel{\approx}
-BetaRT
is similar to
\mathrel{\approx}
-BetaTR
, so among the two we only consider the latter. Congruence case
Congruence under an abstraction
follows from P1 (generalized
\mathcal{F}
-Abs
), P2 and the IH. Congruence case
Congruence under a composition
follows from the IH.
This leaves the following cases:

1.

\mathrel{\approx}
-IdL
.

Let
\underline{\rho^{\mathsf{src}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho\mathrel{\approx}\rho
.
Let
\rho^{\mathcal{F}}=\mathtt{K}\langle\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}\rangle
.
Note that
(\rho^{\mathcal{F}})^{\mathsf{src}}=\mu_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}
.
Then:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}(\underline{\rho^{\mathsf{src}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho)^{\mathcal{F}}&=&(\underline{\rho^{\mathsf{src}}})^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho^{\mathcal{F}}\\
&=&(\underline{(\rho^{\mathcal{F}})^{\mathsf{src}}})^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho^{\mathcal{F}}&\text{since $\underline{(\rho^{\mathcal{F}})^{\mathsf{src}}}\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}(\underline{\rho^{\mathsf{src}}})^{\mathcal{F}}$
}\\
&=&(\underline{\mu_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}})^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\rho^{\mathcal{F}}&\text{since $(\rho^{\mathcal{F}})^{\mathsf{src}}=\mu_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}$}\\
&=&(\underline{\mu_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}})^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathtt{K}\langle\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}\rangle\\
&=&\mathtt{K}\langle((\underline{\mu_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}})^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mu_{1}),\ldots,\mu_{n}\rangle&\text{by {{$\mathrel{\sim}$-Assoc}}}\\
&\mathrel{\sim}&\mathtt{K}\langle\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}\rangle&\text{since $\mu_{1}\Leftrightarrow\underline{\mu_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mu_{1}$}\\
&=&\rho^{\mathcal{F}}\end{array}

In the second step above, note that
\underline{(\rho^{\mathcal{F}})^{\mathsf{src}}}\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}(\underline{\rho^{\mathsf{src}}})^{\mathcal{F}}
follows from the fact that
steps other than
\mathcal{F}
-BetaM
and
\mathcal{F}
-EtaM

preserve the endpoints,
while

\mathcal{F}
-BetaM
and
\mathcal{F}
-EtaM
reduction steps commute
with taking the endpoints.
Hence we have that

\underline{\rho^{\mathsf{src}}}\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}\underline{(\rho^{\mathcal{F}})^{\mathsf{src}}}
and we conclude by confluence of flattening.

2.

\mathrel{\approx}
-App
.

Let
(\rho_{1}\,\rho_{2})\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2})\mathrel{\approx}(\rho_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma_{1})\,(\rho_{2}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma_{2})
.
Consider the
\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}
-normal forms of each rewrite:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\rho_{1}^{\mathcal{F}}=\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}\rangle&\rho_{2}^{\mathcal{F}}=\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{m}\rangle\\
\sigma_{1}^{\mathcal{F}}=\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{1}\langle\hat{\mu}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mu}_{p}\rangle&\sigma_{2}^{\mathcal{F}}=\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{2}\langle\hat{\nu}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\nu}_{q}\rangle\end{array}

Before going on, we make the following claim:

\mu_{n}^{\blacktriangleright}=\hat{\mu}_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{ and }\hskip 14.22636pt\nu_{m}^{\blacktriangleright}=\hat{\nu}_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft}\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt\text{$(\star)$}

For the first equality,
note that
\rho_{1}
and
\sigma_{1}
are composable,
so
\rho_{1}^{\mathsf{tgt}}=_{\beta\eta}\sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{src}}

are
\beta\eta
-equivalent terms.
Moreover, by Remark
21

and Lemma
25

we have that
(\rho_{1}^{\mathcal{F}})^{\mathsf{tgt}}=_{\beta\eta}\rho_{1}^{\mathsf{tgt}}

and
(\sigma_{1}^{\mathcal{F}})^{\mathsf{src}}=_{\beta\eta}\sigma_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}
.
This means that
\mu_{n}^{\mathsf{tgt}}=_{\beta\eta}\hat{\mu}_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}
,
so by confluence and strong normalization of flattening

\mu_{n}^{\blacktriangleright}=\hat{\mu}_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft}
.
Similarly, for the second equality,
since
\rho_{2}
and
\sigma_{2}
are composable,
we have that
\nu_{m}^{\blacktriangleright}=\hat{\nu}_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft}
.

Furthermore, we claim that the two following conditions hold:

(I)

\rho_{1}\,\rho_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}\mathrel{\sim}\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle(\mu_{1}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\mu_{n}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft}\,\nu_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft}\,\nu_{m})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle

(II)

\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}\mathrel{\sim}\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{1}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{1}\,\nu_{m}^{\blacktriangleright})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{p}\,\nu_{m}^{\blacktriangleright})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{2}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\hat{\nu}_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\hat{\nu}_{q})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle

To prove
(I)
, note that:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}&&\rho_{1}\,\rho_{2}\\
&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}\mathrel{\sim}&\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle(\mu_{1}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\mu_{n}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle(\mu_{n}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\nu_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\mu_{n}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\nu_{m})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle&\text{by P3 above}\\
&=&\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle(\mu_{1}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\mu_{n}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft}\,\nu_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft}\,\nu_{m})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle&\text{by the claim $(\star)$ above}\end{array}

The proof of
(II)
is symmetric to the proof of
(I)
.

To conclude the proof of the
\mathrel{\sim}
-App
case,
let us rewrite the left-hand side.
We use the associativity rule (
\mathrel{\sim}
-Assoc
) implicitly:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}&&(\rho_{1}\,\rho_{2})\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}(\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2})\\
&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}\mathrel{\sim}&\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle(\mu_{1}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\mu_{n}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft}\,\nu_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft}\,\nu_{m})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\\
&&\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{1}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{1}\,\nu_{m}^{\blacktriangleright})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{p}\,\nu_{m}^{\blacktriangleright})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{2}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\hat{\nu}_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\hat{\nu}_{q})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\\
&&\hskip 14.22636pt\text{by claims {\bf(I)} and {\bf(II)}}\\
&\mathrel{\sim}&\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle(\mu_{1}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\mu_{n}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{1}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{1}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{p}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\\
&&\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\nu_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\nu_{m})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{2}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\hat{\nu}_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\hat{\nu}_{q})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\\
&&\hskip 14.22636pt\text{($\star$)}\end{array}

The
(\star)
above refers to the following swapping property which can proved by induction on
\mathtt{K}_{1}
:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}&\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle(\mu_{1}\,\underline{\nu_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\mu_{n}\,\underline{\nu_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle(\underline{\mu_{n}^{\mathsf{tgt}}}\,\nu_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\underline{\mu_{n}^{\mathsf{tgt}}}\,\nu_{m})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\\
\mathrel{\sim}&\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle(\underline{\mu_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}}\,\nu_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\underline{\mu_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}}\,\nu_{m})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle(\mu_{1}\,\underline{\nu_{m}^{\mathsf{tgt}}})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\mu_{n}\,\underline{\nu_{m}^{\mathsf{tgt}}})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\end{array}

where
\mathtt{K}_{1},\mathtt{K}_{2}
are arbitrary composition trees
and

\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n},\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{m}
are arbitrary multisteps.

On the other hand, rewriting the right-hand side:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}&&(\rho_{1}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma_{1})\,(\rho_{2}\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\sigma_{2})\\
&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}&(\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{1}\langle\hat{\mu}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mu}_{p}\rangle)\,(\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{m}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{2}\langle\hat{\nu}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\nu}_{q}\rangle)\\
&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}&\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle(\mu_{1}\,\underline{\nu_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}}),\ldots,(\mu_{n}\,\underline{\nu_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}})\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{1}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{1}\,\underline{\nu_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}}),\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{p}\,\underline{\nu_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}})\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\\
&&\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle(\underline{\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\mathsf{tgt}}}\,\nu_{1}),\ldots,(\underline{\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\mathsf{tgt}}}\,\nu_{m})\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{2}\langle(\underline{\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\mathsf{tgt}}}\,\hat{\nu}_{1}),\ldots,(\underline{\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\mathsf{tgt}}}\,\hat{\nu}_{q})\rangle\\
&&\hskip 14.22636pt\text{by P1 (generalized
{{$\mathcal{F}$-App3}})}\\
&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}&\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle(\mu_{1}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\mu_{n}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{1}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{1}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{p}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\\
&&\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\nu_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\nu_{m})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{2}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\hat{\nu}_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\hat{\nu}_{q})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\end{array}

3.

\mathrel{\approx}
-BetaTR
.

Let
(\lambda x.\underline{s})\,\rho\mathrel{\approx}s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}
,
and suppose that
\rho^{\mathcal{F}}=\mathtt{K}\langle\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}\rangle
.
First note that,

(\lambda x.\underline{s})\,\rho\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}\mathtt{K}\langle((\lambda x.\underline{s})\,\mu_{1}),\ldots,((\lambda x.\underline{s})\,\mu_{n})\rangle
.
Indeed, if
n=1
this is immediate, and if
n>1

this is a consequence of P1 (generalized
\mathcal{F}
-App2
). Hence:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}(\lambda x.\underline{s})\,\rho&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}&\mathtt{K}\langle((\lambda x.\underline{s})\,\mu_{1}),\ldots,((\lambda x.\underline{s})\,\mu_{n})\rangle\\
&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}&\mathtt{K}\langle s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\{}}}x\backslash\mu_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\}}}},\ldots,s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\{}}}x\backslash\mu_{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bm{\}}}}\rangle&\text{by {{$\mathcal{F}$-BetaM}} ($n$ times)}\\
&=&\mathtt{K}\langle s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\mu_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}},\ldots,s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\mu_{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\rangle&\text{by Remark~\ref{remark:multistep_substitution_vs_subtr_subrt}(2)}\\
&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}&\mathtt{K}\langle s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\mu_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}},\ldots,s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\mu_{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}\rangle^{\mathcal{F}}\\
&\mathrel{\sim}\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsfrom}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}&s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\mathtt{K}\langle\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}&\text{by P4}\\
&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsfrom}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}&s{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\{}}}x{\backslash}\!\!{\backslash}\rho{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\bm{\}}}}&\text{by Lemma~\ref{lemma:flattening_below_subs}(1)}\end{array}

4.

\mathrel{\approx}
-Eta
.

Let
\lambda x.\rho\,x\mathrel{\approx}\rho

where
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(\rho)
.
Let
\rho^{\mathcal{F}}=\mathtt{K}\langle\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}\rangle
.
It suffices to note that

(\lambda x.\rho\,x)^{\mathcal{F}}=\rho^{\mathcal{F}}
. Indeed:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}\lambda x.\rho\,x&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}&\lambda x.\rho^{\mathcal{F}}\,x\\
&=&\lambda x.\mathtt{K}\langle\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}\rangle\,x\\
&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}&\lambda x.\mathtt{K}\langle(\mu_{1}\,x),\ldots,(\mu_{n}\,x)\rangle&\text{by P1 (generalized
{{$\mathcal{F}$-App1}})}\\
&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}&\mathtt{K}\langle\lambda x.(\mu_{1}\,x),\ldots,\lambda x.(\mu_{n}\,x)\rangle&\text{by P1 (generalized
{{$\mathcal{F}$-Abs}})}\\
&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}&\mathtt{K}\langle\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}\rangle&\text{by {{$\mathcal{F}$-EtaM}} ($n$ times)}\\
&=&\rho^{\mathcal{F}}\end{array}

Note that we may apply the
\mathcal{F}
-EtaM
rule
because, for each
1\leq i\leq n
,
we have that
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(\mu_{i})
.
This in turn is justified by noting that

x\notin\mathsf{fv}(\mathtt{K}\langle\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}\rangle)=\mathsf{fv}(\rho^{\mathcal{F}})
,
which is a consequence of the fact that
flattening does not create free variables.

5.

Congruence under an application.

Let
\rho_{1}\,\rho_{2}\mathrel{\approx}\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}

be derived from
\rho_{1}\mathrel{\approx}\sigma_{1}

and
\rho_{2}\mathrel{\approx}\sigma_{2}
.
Consider the
\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}}
-normal forms of each rewrite:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\rho_{1}^{\mathcal{F}}=\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}\rangle&\rho_{2}^{\mathcal{F}}=\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{m}\rangle\\
\sigma_{1}^{\mathcal{F}}=\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{1}\langle\hat{\mu}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mu}_{p}\rangle&\sigma_{2}^{\mathcal{F}}=\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{2}\langle\hat{\nu}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\nu}_{q}\rangle\end{array}

By IH we have that

\rho_{1}^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{\sim}\sigma_{1}^{\mathcal{F}}

and

\rho_{2}^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{\sim}\sigma_{2}^{\mathcal{F}}
.
Before going on, we make the following claim:

\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft}=\hat{\nu}_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{ and }\hskip 14.22636pt\mu_{n}^{\blacktriangleleft}=\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\blacktriangleleft}\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt(\star)

For the first equality, note that

\rho_{2}\mathrel{\approx}\sigma_{2}
,
so
\rho_{2}^{\mathsf{src}}=_{\beta\eta}\sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{src}}

are
\beta\eta
-equivalent terms by Lemma
13
.
Moreover, by Remark
21

and Lemma
25

we have that
(\rho_{2}^{\mathcal{F}})^{\mathsf{src}}=_{\beta\eta}\rho_{2}^{\mathsf{src}}

and
(\sigma_{2}^{\mathcal{F}})^{\mathsf{src}}=_{\beta\eta}\sigma_{2}^{\mathsf{src}}
.
This means that
\nu_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}=_{\beta\eta}\hat{\nu}_{1}^{\mathsf{src}}
,
so by confluence and strong normalization of flattening

\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft}=\hat{\nu}_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft}
.
Similarly, for the second equality,
since
\rho_{1}\mathrel{\approx}\sigma_{1}
,
we have that
\mu_{n}^{\blacktriangleright}=\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\blacktriangleright}
.
Then:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}&&\rho_{1}\,\rho_{2}\\
&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}\mathrel{\sim}&\mathtt{K}_{1}\langle(\mu_{1}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\mu_{n}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle(\mu_{n}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\nu_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\mu_{n}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\nu_{m})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle&\text{by P3}\\
&\mathrel{\sim}&\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{1}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{1}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{p}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\mathtt{K}_{2}\langle(\mu_{n}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\nu_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\mu_{n}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\nu_{m})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle&\text{by P2 (as $\rho_{1}^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{\sim}\sigma_{1}^{\mathcal{F}}$)}\\
&\mathrel{\sim}&\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{1}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{1}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{p}\,\nu_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{2}\langle(\mu_{n}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\hat{\nu}_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\mu_{n}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\hat{\nu}_{q})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle&\text{by P2 (as $\rho_{2}^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{\sim}\sigma_{2}^{\mathcal{F}}$)}\\
&=&\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{1}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{1}\,\hat{\nu}_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{p}\,\hat{\nu}_{1}^{\blacktriangleleft})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle\mathrel{{\bm{;}}}\tilde{\mathtt{K}}_{2}\langle(\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\hat{\nu}_{1})^{\mathcal{F}},\ldots,(\hat{\mu}_{p}^{\blacktriangleright}\,\hat{\nu}_{q})^{\mathcal{F}}\rangle&\text{by the claim $(\star)$ above}\\
&\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsfrom}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}&\sigma_{1}\,\sigma_{2}&\text{by P3}\end{array}

Theorem 36

(Soundness and completeness of flat permutation equivalence)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\rho}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\sigma}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t^{\prime}}:A
.
Then
\rho\mathrel{\approx}\sigma

if and only if
\rho^{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{\sim}\sigma^{\mathcal{F}}
.

6.
Projection

This section presents projection equivalence.
Two rewrites
\rho
and
\sigma
are said to be projection equivalent if the
steps performed by
\rho
are included in those performed by
\sigma

and vice-versa. We proceed in stages as follows.
First, we define
projection
of
multisteps over multisteps (Definition
6.1
)
and prove some of its properties (Proposition
40
).
Second, we extend
projection to flat rewrites (Definition
6.2
).
Third, we extend
projection to arbitrary rewrites (Definition
6.3
)
and, again, we prove some of its properties (Proposition
55
).
Finally, we show that the induced notion of
projection equivalence

turns out to coincide with
permutation equivalence (Theorem
56
).

6.1.
Projection for Multisteps.

Consider the rewrites
\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta
and
\varrho\,\mathbf{f}
, using the notation of Example
4
, each representing one step. Since rewrites are subject to
\beta\eta
-equivalence, to define projection one must ‘‘line up’’ rule symbols with the left-hand side of the rewrite rules they witness
4
4

4

See also the discussion on p. 120 of
[
Bru08
]
.
. For example, if the above two multisteps were rewritten as
(\lambda y.\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.y\,x))\,\vartheta
and
\varrho\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x)
, respectively, then one can reason inductively as follows to compute the projection of the former over the latter (the inference rules themselves are introduced in Definition
6.1
):

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\begin{array}[]{c}\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 91.71106pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vphantom{\vdash}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=189.53323pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{\small{ProjRuleR}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lambda y.\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.y\,x)/\!\!/\!\!/\varrho\Rightarrow\lambda y.y\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.y\,x))$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 31.85875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vphantom{\vdash}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=69.82861pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{\small{ProjRuleL}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vartheta/\!\!/\!\!/\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x\Rightarrow\vartheta$}}}}\end{array}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=366.90977pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{\small{ProjApp}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 55.99612pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(\lambda y.\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.y\,x))\,\vartheta/\!\!/\!\!/\varrho\,(\lambda x.\mathbf{f}\,x)\Rightarrow(\lambda y.y\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.y\,x)))\,\vartheta$}}}}\end{array}

The flat normal form of
(\lambda y.y\,(\mathbf{mu}\,(\lambda x.y\,x)))\,\vartheta

is the rewrite

\vartheta\,(\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta)
. Hence we would deduce

\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta/\!\!/\!\!/\varrho\,\mathbf{f}\Rightarrow\vartheta\,(\mathbf{mu}\,\vartheta)
.
We begin by introducing an auxiliary notion of projection on coinitial multisteps that may not be flat (
i.e.
may not be in
\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-BetaM}},\textsf{{$\mathcal{F}$-EtaM}}
-normal form) called
weak projection
. We then make use of this notion, to define projection for flat multisteps (Definition
6.1
).

{defi}

[Weak projection and compatibility]

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\mu}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}t}:A

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.47,0.27,0.23}\nu}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}s^{\prime}}\rightarrowtriangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}r}:A

be multisteps, not necessarily in normal form,
such that
s=_{\beta\eta}s^{\prime}
.
The judgment
\mu/\!\!/\!\!/\nu\Rightarrow\xi

is defined as follows:

{\small\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 23.1075pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=46.215pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{ProjVar}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x/\!\!/\!\!/x\Rightarrow x$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 17.71788pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=35.43576pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{ProjCon}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbf{c}/\!\!/\!\!/\mathbf{c}\Rightarrow\mathbf{c}$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 26.49031pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=52.98062pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{ProjRule}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\varrho/\!\!/\!\!/\varrho\Rightarrow\varrho^{\mathsf{tgt}}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 25.03935pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=50.0787pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{ProjRuleL}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\varrho/\!\!/\!\!/\varrho^{\mathsf{src}}\Rightarrow\varrho$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 29.3963pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=58.7926pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{ProjRuleR}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\varrho^{\mathsf{src}}/\!\!/\!\!/\varrho\Rightarrow\varrho^{\mathsf{tgt}}$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 22.12488pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\mu/\!\!/\!\!/\nu\Rightarrow\xi$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=88.92648pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{ProjAbs}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lambda x.\mu/\!\!/\!\!/\lambda x.\nu\Rightarrow\lambda x.\xi$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\mu_{1}/\!\!/\!\!/\nu_{1}\Rightarrow\xi_{1}\hskip 14.22636pt\mu_{2}/\!\!/\!\!/\nu_{2}\Rightarrow\xi_{2}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.54375pt,depth=-2.08122pt,width=120.42955pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\textsf{{ProjApp}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 17.79362pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu_{1}\,\mu_{2}/\!\!/\!\!/\nu_{1}\,\nu_{2}\Rightarrow\xi_{1}\,\xi_{2}$}}}}\end{array}\end{array}}

We say that
\mu
and
\nu
are
compatible
,
written
\mu\uparrow\nu

if, intuitively speaking,

\mu
and
\nu
are coinitial,
and are “almost”
\eta
-expanded and
\beta
-normal forms,
with the exception that
the head of the term may be the source of a rule,

i.e.
a term of the form
\varrho^{\mathsf{src}}
.
Compatibility is defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 10.85437pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty(\mu_{i}\uparrow\nu_{i})_{i=1}^{m}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=68.34247pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{CVar}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lambda\vec{x}.y\,\vec{\mu}\uparrow\lambda\vec{x}.y\,\vec{\nu}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 9.03723pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty(\mu_{i}\uparrow\nu_{i})_{i=1}^{m}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=64.70819pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{CCon}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lambda\vec{x}.\mathbf{c}\,\vec{\mu}\uparrow\lambda\vec{x}.\mathbf{c}\,\vec{\nu}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 10.76292pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty(\mu_{i}\uparrow\nu_{i})_{i=1}^{m}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=68.15958pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{CRule}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lambda\vec{x}.\varrho\,\vec{\mu}\uparrow\lambda\vec{x}.\varrho\,\vec{\nu}$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 15.10599pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty(\mu_{i}\uparrow\nu_{i})_{i=1}^{m}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=76.8457pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{CRuleL}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lambda\vec{x}.\varrho\,\vec{\mu}\uparrow\lambda\vec{x}.\varrho^{\mathsf{src}}\,\vec{\nu}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 15.10599pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty(\mu_{i}\uparrow\nu_{i})_{i=1}^{m}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=76.8457pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsf{{CRuleR}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lambda\vec{x}.\varrho^{\mathsf{src}}\,\vec{\mu}\uparrow\lambda\vec{x}.\varrho\,\vec{\nu}$}}}}\end{array}\end{array}

The interesting cases are the two last rules, which state essentially
that a rule symbol is compatible with its source term.
Clearly if
\mu\uparrow\nu
, then there exists a unique
\xi
such that
\mu/\!\!/\!\!/\nu\Rightarrow\xi
.
Moreover, weak projection is coherent with respect to flattening:

Lemma 37

(Coherence of projection)
.

Let
\mu_{1},\nu_{1},\mu_{2},\nu_{2}
be multisteps
such that

\mu_{1}\uparrow\nu_{1}
,

\mu_{2}\uparrow\nu_{2}
,

\mu_{1}^{\mathcal{F}}=\mu_{2}^{\mathcal{F}}
,

\nu_{1}^{\mathcal{F}}=\nu_{2}^{\mathcal{F}}
,

\mu_{1}/\!\!/\!\!/\nu_{1}\Rightarrow\xi_{1}
,
and

\mu_{2}/\!\!/\!\!/\nu_{2}\Rightarrow\xi_{2}
.
Then
\xi_{1}^{\mathcal{F}}=\xi_{2}^{\mathcal{F}}
.

Proof 6.1
.

The proof proceeds by induction on the
derivation of
\mu_{1}\uparrow\nu_{1}
. Note that the last rule used
to derive
\mu_{2}\uparrow\nu_{2}

must be the same as the last rule used
to derive
\mu_{1}\uparrow\nu_{1}
.
The core of the argument in these cases is to apply the IH using the following
matching property
:
If
(\varrho^{\mathsf{src}}\,\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n})^{\mathcal{F}}=(\varrho^{\mathsf{src}}\,\nu_{1}\ldots\nu_{n})^{\mathcal{F}}

then
\mu_{i}^{\mathcal{F}}=\nu_{i}^{\mathcal{F}}

for all
1\leq i\leq n
. This property is well-known and a direct consequence of the assumption that LHS of rewrite rule are rule-patterns.

Lemma 38

(Compatibilization of coinitial multisteps)
.

Let
\mu,\nu
be multisteps
such that
\mu^{\blacktriangleleft}=\nu^{\blacktriangleleft}
.
Then there exist multisteps
\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu}

such that
\hat{\mu}\uparrow\hat{\nu}

and moreover
\hat{\mu}^{\mathcal{F}}=\mu^{\mathcal{F}}

and
\hat{\nu}^{\mathcal{F}}=\nu^{\mathcal{F}}
.

Proof 6.2
.

We begin with a few observations.
Recall that if
\mu\mathrel{\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\mapsto}{\hskip-1.99997pt}^{*}}\mu^{\prime}

then
\mu^{\mathsf{src}}=_{\beta\eta}\mu^{\prime\mathsf{src}}
,
which is a consequence of
Remark
21

and Lemma
25
.
This means that, without loss of generality, we may assume that

\mu,\nu
are in
\mathrel{\overset{\circ}{\mapsto}},\overline{\eta}
-normal form.
Note that, given this assumption,
we have that
\mu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}}
and
\mu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}}
are
in
\overline{\eta}
-normal form.
Moreover,

(\mu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\mathcal{F}}=(\mu^{\mathsf{src}})^{\mathcal{F}}=(\nu^{\mathsf{src}})^{\mathcal{F}}=(\nu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\mathcal{F}}
.
From this,
we have that

(\mu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}=(\nu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}
.

The proof proceeds by induction
on the sum of the number of applications in
\mu
and
\nu
,
using a straightforward characterization of flat multisteps. We consider three cases, depending on whether the head of
\mu

is a variable, a constant, or a rule symbol:

1.

Variable,

\mu=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.y\,\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{m}
.

Note that

(\mu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.y\,(\mu_{1}^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}\ldots(\mu_{m}^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}
.
Since
(\mu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}=(\nu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}
,
the head of
\nu
cannot be a constant or a rule symbol.
Hence the only possibility is that

\nu=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{p}.y\,\nu_{1}\ldots\nu_{q}
.
Furthermore, it can only be the case that
p=n
and
q=m

and
(\mu_{i}^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}=(\nu_{i}^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}

for all
1\leq i\leq m
.
This in turn implies that

\mu_{i}^{\blacktriangleleft}=\nu_{i}^{\blacktriangleleft}
for all
1\leq i\leq m
.
Hence, by IH, for each
1\leq i\leq m
,
there exist multisteps such that

\hat{\mu}_{i}\uparrow\hat{\nu}_{i}

where
\hat{\mu}_{i}^{\mathcal{F}}=\mu_{i}^{\mathcal{F}}

and
\hat{\nu}_{i}^{\mathcal{F}}=\nu_{i}^{\mathcal{F}}
.
To conclude, note that
taking

\hat{\mu}:=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.y\,\hat{\mu}_{1}\ldots\hat{\mu}_{m}

and

\hat{\nu}:=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.y\,\hat{\nu}_{1}\ldots\hat{\nu}_{m}

we have that

\hat{\mu}\uparrow\hat{\nu}
by the
CVar
rule,
and moreover

\hat{\mu}^{\mathcal{F}}=\mu^{\mathcal{F}}

and

\hat{\nu}^{\mathcal{F}}=\nu^{\mathcal{F}}
.

2.

Constant,

\mu=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\mathbf{c}\,\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{m}
.

Note that

(\mu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\mathbf{c}\,(\mu_{1}^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}\ldots(\mu_{m}^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}
.
Since
(\nu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}=(\mu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}
,
the head of
\nu
cannot be a variable.
We consider two cases, depending on whether the head of
\nu

is a constant or a rule symbol:

2.1

Constant,

\nu=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{p}.\mathbf{c}\,\nu_{1}\ldots\nu_{q}
.

The proof of this case proceeds similarly
as for case
1
,
when the heads of
\mu
and
\nu
are both variables.

2.2

Rule symbol,

\nu=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{p}.\varrho\,\nu_{1}\ldots\nu_{q}
.

Recall that we assume that
\mu
and
\nu
are
in
\overline{\eta}
-normal form. This implies that
n=p
.
Then

(\nu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}=(\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\varrho^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}}\,\nu_{1}^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}}\ldots\nu_{q}^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}
.
By Lemma
45
, there exist multisteps

\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{q}

such that

\mu=(\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\varrho^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}}\,\xi_{1}\ldots\xi_{q})^{\circ}

and
(\xi_{i}^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}=(\nu_{i}^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}

for all
1\leq i\leq q
.
Moreover, each
\xi_{i}
has less applications than
\mu
.
So by IH, for each
1\leq i\leq q

there exist multisteps such that

\hat{\xi}_{i}\uparrow\hat{\nu}_{i}

where

\hat{\xi}_{i}^{\mathcal{F}}=\xi_{i}^{\mathcal{F}}
.
and

\hat{\nu}_{i}^{\mathcal{F}}=\nu_{i}^{\mathcal{F}}
.
Taking

\hat{\mu}:=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\varrho^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}}\,\hat{\xi}_{1}\ldots\hat{\xi}_{q}

and

\hat{\nu}:=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\varrho\,\hat{\nu}_{1}\ldots\hat{\nu}_{q}

we have that
\hat{\mu}\uparrow\hat{\nu}
.
It is easy to note that
\hat{\nu}^{\mathcal{F}}=\nu^{\mathcal{F}}
.
Moreover:

\begin{array}[]{rlll}\hat{\mu}^{\mathcal{F}}&=&(\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\varrho^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}}\,\hat{\xi}_{1}\ldots\hat{\xi}_{q})^{\mathcal{F}}\\
&=&(\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\varrho^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}}\,\xi_{1}\ldots\xi_{q})^{\mathcal{F}}&\text{by confluence of flattening~(Proposition~\ref{prop:flat_confluent})}\\
&=&((\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\varrho^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}}\,\xi_{1}\ldots\xi_{q})^{\circ})^{\mathcal{F}}&\text{by confluence of flattening~(Proposition~\ref{prop:flat_confluent})}\\
&=&\mu^{\mathcal{F}}\end{array}

3.

Rule symbol,

\mu=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.\varrho\,\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{m}
.

Note that

(\mu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.(\varrho^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}}\,\mu_{1}^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}}\ldots\mu_{m}^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}
.
Since
(\nu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}=(\mu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}
,
the head of
\nu
cannot be a variable.
We consider two cases, depending on whether the head of
\nu

is a constant or a rule symbol:

3.1

Constant,

\nu=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{p}.\mathbf{c}\,\nu_{1}\ldots\nu_{q}
.

The proof of this case proceeds symmetrically
as for case
2.2
,
when the head of
\mu
is a constant
and the head of
\nu
is a rule symbol.

3.2

Rule symbol,

\nu=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{p}.\vartheta\,\nu_{1}\ldots\nu_{q}
.

Recall that we assume that
\mu
and
\nu
are in

\overline{\eta}
-normal form. This implies that
n=p
.
Then

(\nu^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.(\vartheta^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}}\,\nu_{1}^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}}\ldots\nu_{q}^{\mathsf{<\overline{\eta}}})^{\circ}
.
Note that this implies that
